## BASIC LEARNING SESSION

### Exact printed ownership and cross-topic firewall

The printed clause owns the **conceptual, epistemic, motivational, sanctioning and sociological relations between religion and morality**, together with their mutual criticism and the Indian–Western comparison of moral grounds.

| Owned here | Boundary that is not crossed |
|---|---|
| whether God or religion grounds moral truth or moral obligation | the moral argument *for God's existence* belongs to the Proofs for God owner |
| faith, conscience, guilt, love, salvation and moral commitment **as religion–morality links** | the complete epistemology of faith and revelation belongs to the Reason, Revelation and Faith owner |
| morality without God, and the non-theistic ethical systems that ground it | the definition and possibility of *religion* without God belongs to the Religion without God owner |
| conflicting religious moral interpretations, treated as a problem of moral criticism | full pluralism and absolute-truth theory belongs to the Religious Pluralism owner |
| Indian duty, cosmic order, karma, non-injury and compassion **as comparative moral grounds** | complete Indian ethics and liberation doctrine belongs to the Paper I and Ethics owners |
| religious violence, and the just war / *jihād* / *dharma-yuddha* comparison, **only because of the printed 2019 question** | descriptive conflict history, political ideology and detailed juristic doctrine are not substitutes for premise-testing |

⚠️ **[FACT]** The firewall that governs the whole clause: **sociological influence does not establish philosophical dependence.** Religion may motivate or sanction conduct without constituting moral truth, and morality may criticise a religious command without denying every religious role. Almost every weak script on this clause collapses those two claims into one sentence and never recovers.

### 0A. FOUR DIFFERENT DEPENDENCE CLAIMS

```text
        HOW ARE RELIGION AND MORALITY RELATED?
   ┌────────────────┬──────────────────┬────────────────────┐
     DEPENDENCE        INDEPENDENCE        INTERACTION
     morality NEEDS    morality is         they mutually
     religion / God    AUTONOMOUS          influence and refine
     (divine command   (Kant: reason;      (religion motivates;
      theory)           secular ethics)     morality purifies)
          │                  │                     │
   EUTHYPHRO DILEMMA (Plato, Euthyphro 10a):
   Is the pious loved by the gods because it is pious,
   or pious because it is loved?
      ↳ horn 1 → morality is ARBITRARY
      ↳ horn 2 → goodness is INDEPENDENT of God
```

> 🔑 **Mnemonic — "Depend / Detach / Dialogue":** morality *depends* on religion (divine command), *detaches* from it (Kant and secular ethics), or *dialogues* with it (interaction). The Euthyphro splits the first option down the middle.

**[FACT] The four claims that must never be merged.** The single most valuable table in this clause:

| Claim | The question it answers | Religious role asserted | Independent alternative |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Metaphysical grounding** | What *makes* a moral truth or obligation valid? | divine nature, divine command, or a created natural order | moral realism, rational autonomy, flourishing, contractual or non-theistic grounds |
| **Moral knowledge (epistemic)** | How do agents *know* what is right? | revelation, scripture, conscience, tradition and exemplars | reason, experience, empathy, public argument |
| **Motivation** | Why act morally when it is *costly or unseen*? | love, gratitude, hope, repentance, salvation, community and divine relationship | duty, character, solidarity, compassion, self-respect |
| **Sanction and accountability** | What *connects* conduct with reward, guilt or consequence? | judgement, sin, forgiveness, karma or eschatological consequence | law, social response, internal conscience, causal consequences |

**[FACT]** Success in one role proves no dependence in another. A religion may strengthen motivation while conceding independently knowable moral truth; a secular ethic may justify duty impeccably while struggling to move every agent in every case.

### Local source and factual control

- Local searchable John Hick, *Philosophy of Religion*, especially PDF pp. 23–24 and 39–40, controls the conceptual independence of ethics and the links to the moral argument.
- The local searchable *Oxford Handbook of Philosophy of Religion*, "Morality and Religion," print pp. 344–365 (approximately PDF pp. 356–377), controls divine command, natural law, motivation and autonomy throughout.
- Local searchable Chatterjee–Datta, *An Introduction to Indian Philosophy*, especially PDF pp. 71–73 for Mīmāṃsā duty and pp. 141–143 for Jain non-injury; local C. D. Sharma supplies the bounded *Bhagavad Gītā* and Indian ethical comparisons.
- **[FACT]** No opinion survey, crime statistic or contemporary sociological correlation is used anywhere in this package to settle a philosophical question. Where behavioural evidence appears, it is used only to illustrate *influence*, never to establish *dependence*.

### Origin, chronology and anachronism control

A calendar timeline is not the natural shape of this clause: it is a dispute about a *relation between two concepts*, not an institution with a founding date. What is required is the minimum chronology that prevents anachronism, plus the dated print anchors an examiner can be quoted at.

```text
CONCEPTUAL ORIGIN OF THE PROBLEM
  Stage 1  Morality is presented as the content of a divine will.
  Stage 2  Plato asks whether the divine will TRACKS goodness or CREATES it.
           ==> the dilemma is older than every theory built to escape it.
  Stage 3  Escape upward: goodness is the divine NATURE, not a decree (natural law;
           later, modified divine command).
  Stage 4  Escape sideways: obligation is rational SELF-legislation (Kant).
  Stage 5  Escape outward: whole moral systems are built with no commander at all
           (Mimamsa injunction, Buddhist intention, Jain vows, secular theories).
  Stage 6  The question turns back on religion: does religiosity IMPROVE conduct,
           and can a religious premise ever license violence?
           ==> the clause exists because Stages 3-5 succeed only partially,
               and Stage 6 cannot be settled by any of them.

DATED PRINT ANCHORS  (assert these; invent no others)
  c. 399-390 BCE  Plato, Euthyphro 10a  — the dilemma, in both horns
  c. 380-375 BCE  Plato, Republic II 359d-360b — Glaucon's Ring of Gyges
  1651            Thomas Hobbes, Leviathan — the Foole
  1689            John Locke, A Letter Concerning Toleration — belief cannot be
                  compelled by outward force
  1751            David Hume, Enquiry Concerning the Principles of Morals IX.ii
                  — the sensible knave
  1876            F. H. Bradley, Ethical Studies, Essay II, "Why Should I Be Moral?"
  1887            Friedrich Nietzsche, On the Genealogy of Morality
  1912            H. A. Prichard, "Does Moral Philosophy Rest on a Mistake?", Mind
  1912            Max Scheler, Ressentiment
  1977            Michael Walzer, Just and Unjust Wars
  1999            Robert Merrihew Adams, Finite and Infinite Goods

CITE BY WORK, NEVER BY AN INVENTED YEAR
  Kant            ->  Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals; Religion within the
                      Bounds of Bare Reason
  Aquinas         ->  Summa Theologiae II-II q.40 (the three war conditions)
  Adams           ->  Finite and Infinite Goods       Zagzebski -> Divine Motivation Theory
  Alston          ->  "Some Suggestions for Divine Command Theorists"
  Gauthier        ->  Morals by Agreement (constrained maximisation)
  Nietzsche       ->  The Gay Science 125; Genealogy I, II, III; Beyond Good and Evil,
                      Preface; The Antichrist; Thus Spoke Zarathustra
  Santideva       ->  Bodhicaryavatara VIII (ownerless suffering)
  Jaimini         ->  Mimamsa-sutra (duty from injunction alone)
  Bhagavad Gita   ->  2.3 (klaibya); 2.42-43; 2.47; 3.19
  Kautilya        ->  Arthasastra (kuta-yuddha, the Indian realist counter-tradition)
  Mahabharata     ->  Santi-parvan and Anusasana-parvan (dharma-yuddha rules)
  Augustine       ->  compelle intrare, Luke 14:23, in the Donatist controversy
  Allport         ->  intrinsic and extrinsic religious orientation
  Batson          ->  the quest orientation

ANACHRONISM CONTROL
  The Euthyphro (c. 399-390 BCE) is not an objection to Christian divine-command
  theory; it is the original statement of a structural problem that later theories
  inherit. Mimamsa's duty-from-injunction is not a "secular ethics": it is
  scripturally grounded and impersonally binding, with no commander who is a person.
  Nietzsche (1887) attacks a Christian-Platonic inheritance the Indian systems never
  possessed, so "the death of God" must not be transplanted into Indian material.
  Locke (1689) precedes every modern toleration argument used here; Walzer (1977) is
  a modern systematisation of Aquinas, not a substitute for him.
```

*The rail explains why the clause exists; the dated strip is the only chronology you may assert. Everything in the third block must be cited by work, never by a manufactured year.*

### Learning roadmap

| Session | Learner objective |
|---:|---|
| 1 | Identify *which* of the four dependence claims a stem is actually asserting |

| 20 | Present Nietzsche's criticism of religion *and* morality as one connected argument |

### SESSION 1 — FOUR QUESTIONS HIDING INSIDE ONE: WHICH DEPENDENCE IS BEING ASSERTED?

#### DEFINITION

**Plain-language definition.** To say "morality depends on religion" can mean four completely different things: that religion *makes* moral claims true; that religion is how we *find out* what is right; that religion is what *moves* us to do it; or that religion is what *attaches consequences* to what we did. These are four claims, not four wordings of one claim.

**Technical definition.** The clause tests four separable relations: **metaphysical grounding** (what constitutes moral truth or obligation), **epistemic access** (how moral knowledge is acquired), **motivational force** (what supplies the operative reason to comply), and **sanction or accountability** (what links conduct to reward, guilt or consequence). A fifth relation — mere **sociological correlation** — is not a dependence claim at all, though it is constantly mistaken for one.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> The question is not whether religion and morality are connected — no one denies that they are — but *which* connection is being asserted; and since grounding, knowledge, motivation and sanction have four different answers, an answer that does not say which one it is discussing has not yet begun.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**metaphysical grounding · epistemic access · motivational dependence · sanction and accountability · sociological correlation · necessity claim · semantic dependence · constitutive versus supportive**

**How to use them:** open every answer in this clause by naming the relation the stem asserts. The examiner is buying discrimination, and the single sentence "grounding, knowing, being moved and being held to account are four questions, not one" converts an average script into a good one before any doctrine has been stated.

#### VISUAL — THE FOUR QUESTIONS, AND THE ONE IMPOSTOR

```text
  ┌──────────────────── "MORALITY DEPENDS ON RELIGION" ────────────────────┐
  │                                                                        │
  │  1. GROUNDING        2. KNOWLEDGE       3. MOTIVATION    4. SANCTION   │
  │  what MAKES it       how do I FIND      what MOVES me    what HOLDS    │
  │  obligatory?         out?               to do it?        me to it?     │
  │  ──────────────      ──────────────     ─────────────    ───────────   │
  │  divine command      revelation,        love, gratitude, judgement,    │
  │  divine nature       scripture,         hope, salvation, sin, karma,   │
  │  created order       conscience,        community        forgiveness   │
  │                      exemplars                                         │
  │       ↓                    ↓                  ↓               ↓        │
  │  ANSWERED BY:        ANSWERED BY:       ANSWERED BY:    ANSWERED BY:   │
  │  realism, autonomy,  reason, experience  duty, character  law, social   │
  │  flourishing,        empathy, public     solidarity,      response,     │
  │  contract            argument            compassion       conscience,   │
  │                                                           consequence   │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
        THE IMPOSTOR:  5. SOCIOLOGICAL CORRELATION
        "religious people give more / behave better / behave worse"
        --> establishes at most a CONTINGENT INFLUENCE.
        It cannot show what makes an act right, who can know it,
        or whether divine command theory survives the Euthyphro.
```

*Read the four columns as four independent questions; read the bottom strip as the mistake that costs the most marks. Winning one column concedes nothing in the other three.*

#### TEACHING

**[FACT] Why the separation is load-bearing.** Success in one role proves no dependence in another. A religion may strengthen motivation while accepting that moral truth is independently knowable; a secular ethic may justify duty rigorously while struggling to motivate an agent whose interest runs the other way. The whole examinable value of this clause lies in refusing to let a victory in one column count as a victory in another.

**[FACT] Grounding.** The grounding question asks what *constitutes* validity. Divine command theory answers: being commanded by God. Natural law answers: participation in an objective order of human goods that is itself grounded in divine reason. Kant answers: rational self-legislation. Non-theistic Indian systems answer variously: Vedic injunction alone (Mīmāṃsā), the causal structure of suffering and intention (Buddhism), the vow-structure of non-injury (Jainism).

**[FACT] Epistemic access.** The epistemic question is about *how we find out*. It is genuinely separable: a norm might be independently true and yet, on a strong epistemic-dependence view, knowable only through revelation. Conversely a theist may hold that the content of morality is fully accessible to unaided reason — which is precisely what the natural-law tradition holds — while still maintaining that God grounds it.

**[FACT] Motivation.** The motivational question asks what supplies the operative reason to comply when compliance is costly. Religion answers with love, gratitude, hope, repentance, the prospect of salvation, community and a relationship with the divine. Secular ethics answers with duty, character, solidarity, compassion and self-respect. Neither answer settles the grounding question.

**[FACT] Sanction and accountability.** The fourth relation is the one candidates most often smuggle into the first. It asks what *connects* conduct with reward, guilt or consequence: judgement, sin, forgiveness, karma and eschatological consequence on the religious side; law, social response, internal conscience and ordinary causal consequences on the other.

**[FACT] Semantic dependence is a fifth and narrower thing.** Within the grounding column, three claims must still be distinguished: **semantic** dependence, that "wrong" *means* "forbidden by God"; **metaphysical** dependence, that wrongness is *constituted* by prohibition even if the word does not mean that; and **motivational** dependence, that belief in God is what makes agents comply. Semantic dependence is the strongest and least defensible of the three.

**[INFERENCE] Constitutive versus supportive.** The distinction that decides the 2022 asymmetry stem is already visible here. If morality is *constitutive* of what we are willing to call a religion, then religion cannot be normatively adequate without it. If religion is at most *supportive* of morality, its removal costs morality a motivational resource and no more. Constitutive and supportive are not two degrees of one relation.

**Objection.** This looks like tidy scholasticism. In lived religion the four relations are fused: the believer does not experience grounding, knowledge, motivation and sanction as four separate things.
**Reply.** **[INFERENCE]** Conceded, and the concession should be made in the answer, because it is true and it shows judgement. But a *philosophical* question about dependence asks what could survive the removal of what — and that question can only be answered by pulling the fused experience apart. The lived fusion is evidence about religious psychology; it is not an argument that the relations are identical.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH

> **Claim:** The proposition "morality depends on religion" is ambiguous between four separable relations, and the stem must be assigned to one before it can be assessed.
> **Named evidence:** Metaphysical grounding is claimed by divine command theory; epistemic access by revelation-based accounts; motivational force by the appeal to love, hope and judgement; sanction by the doctrines of sin, forgiveness and karma — while the alternatives in each column are moral realism, public reason, character and law respectively.
> **Analysis:** Because each column has its own alternative, refuting the grounding claim leaves the motivational claim untouched, and demonstrating religious influence on conduct leaves the grounding claim untouched; the four therefore cannot be argued as one.
> **Qualification:** The separation is analytic rather than phenomenological — in lived religion the four are fused — and an answer that concedes that fusion while insisting on the analytic distinction is stronger than one that pretends the believer experiences four things.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Do not infer logical dependence from sociological influence. This is the single commonest failure in the clause, and it is fatal because it answers a different question from the one printed.
2. ⚠️ Do not reduce the whole debate to divine command theory. The stem may be about knowledge, motivation or sanction, none of which divine command theory is primarily about.
3. ⚠️ Do not treat semantic dependence ("wrong" *means* forbidden) as interchangeable with metaphysical dependence (wrongness is *constituted* by prohibition). The first is far easier to refute and refuting it proves less.

#### MINI RECAP

Four columns — grounding, knowledge, motivation, sanction. One impostor — correlation. One habit — name the column in your first sentence and never let a win in one count as a win in another.

### SESSION 2 — THE DEPENDENCE VIEW: DIVINE COMMAND THEORY STATED PRECISELY

#### DEFINITION

**Plain-language definition.** Divine command theory says that what makes an action obligatory is that God has commanded it. Not that God recommends it, not that God rewards it — that the command is what the obligation *consists in*.

**Technical definition.** **[FACT]** Moral obligation consists in being commanded by God. Modified versions ground the commands in an essentially loving divine nature rather than in bare will. The theory is a claim about the **constitution** of obligation; it is separable from, and much stronger than, the epistemic claim that revelation tells us our duty and the motivational claim that love, judgement or sanction moves us to comply.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Divine command theory is not the pious thought that God approves of good conduct; it is the far stronger claim that the divine command is what obligation *is* — and it is exactly that strength which exposes it to the Euthyphro and which its modified versions spend all their ingenuity trying to retain.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**divine command theory · constitution of obligation · legislator · authority over preference · semantic / metaphysical / motivational dependence · Abrahamic command cases · accountability · heteronomy**

**How to use them:** the phrase to write is "command is *constitutive* of obligation, not merely evidential, motivational or reinforcing". That one clause proves you have the theory rather than a paraphrase of it, and every subsequent objection you raise then lands on the real target.

#### VISUAL — THE ARGUMENT, AND THE THREE STRENGTHS OF THE CLAIM

```text
  THE DIVINE COMMAND ARGUMENT (four steps)
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 1  Obligation has AUTHORITY over mere preference.                      │
  │ 2  A supreme personal LEGISLATOR can issue universally binding commands.│
  │ 3  Divine KNOWLEDGE and GOODNESS secure the right content.             │
  │ 4  ACCOUNTABILITY supplies motivational seriousness.                   │
  │                        ↓                                               │
  │    Therefore: obligation is constituted by divine command.             │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
       PRESUPPOSES: God exists · commands are knowable · authority can
                    generate moral reasons

  THREE STRENGTHS OF "DEPENDS" — weakest to strongest
  ┌──────────────┬───────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────┐
  │ MOTIVATIONAL │ belief in God MOVES   │ true of many agents; proves    │
  │              │ agents to comply      │ nothing about moral truth      │
  ├──────────────┼───────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ METAPHYSICAL │ wrongness is CONSTI-  │ the real thesis; the Euthyphro │
  │              │ TUTED by prohibition  │ is aimed exactly here          │
  ├──────────────┼───────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ SEMANTIC     │ "wrong" MEANS         │ strongest and least defensible:│
  │              │ "forbidden by God"    │ atheists use "wrong" meaningfully│
  └──────────────┴───────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────┘
```

*Read the box for the argument the theory actually makes, and the ladder for the three things "depends" can mean. Aim your objection at the middle rung: the top rung is a straw man and the bottom rung is not the thesis.*

#### TEACHING

**[FACT] The doctrine.** Moral obligation consists in being commanded by God; modified versions ground those commands in an essentially loving divine nature.

**[FACT] The argument, in four steps.** (1) Obligation has authority over preference — it binds whether or not one wants it to. (2) A supreme personal legislator is the kind of being who can issue universally binding commands. (3) Divine knowledge and goodness secure that the content commanded is the right content. (4) Accountability to that legislator supplies motivational seriousness that no merely human convention supplies.

**[FACT] The presuppositions.** ⚠️ Three, and each is contestable: that God exists; that divine commands are *knowable* by fallible agents; and that authority as such can generate moral reasons. The third is the one candidates never state and the one an examiner most rewards, because it is the premise Kant denies outright.

**[FACT] The canonical example.** Abrahamic command cases — a command that appears to override ordinary moral judgement — are the standard test of whether obedience can outrank ethics. They are used in this clause as a *test case* for the structure of the theory, not as a devotional narrative.

**[FACT] What the theory buys.** Objectivity (moral facts are not human constructions), universality (one legislator, one law), authority (obligation outranks preference), and motivational seriousness (accountability). These are real goods and an answer that does not concede them has not stated the position at its strongest.

**[INFERENCE] What the theory costs before Euthyphro is even raised.** Two costs are already visible. First, an **access** cost: if obligation is constituted by command, then agents without reliable access to the command are, strictly, without access to obligation. Second, a **heteronomy** cost: obligation grounded in another will and enforced by accountability is, in Kant's sense, obedience rather than autonomy — a point Session 5 develops.

**Objection.** Divine command theory is not a real position; it is a caricature invented by its critics, and no serious theist holds that God could make cruelty obligatory.
**Reply.** **[INFERENCE]** Half right, and the half that is right should be conceded: the *unmodified* voluntarist version — bare will, unconstrained content — is indeed a limiting case rather than a widely held doctrine. But the position is not a caricature. Its modified forms are defended by named contemporary philosophers, and they are modified precisely to escape the objection, which shows the objection was aimed at something real.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH

> **Claim:** Divine command theory claims that being commanded by God is what moral obligation consists in, and it must be assessed as a constitutive claim rather than as a claim about motives or evidence.
> **Named evidence:** Its four-step argument moves from the authority of obligation over preference, through the capacity of a supreme personal legislator to bind universally, to divine knowledge and goodness as guarantors of content, and finally to accountability as the source of motivational seriousness.
> **Analysis:** So stated, the theory delivers objectivity, universality and authority in one move, which is why it survives centuries of criticism; but it does so by importing three contestable presuppositions — divine existence, knowability of commands, and the power of authority as such to generate moral reasons.
> **Qualification:** The third presupposition, not the first, is where the philosophical action is, because it is the one that Kant denies while granting the others, and an answer that names it has located the real fault line.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Do not state divine command theory as "religion teaches us to be good". That is a claim about moral education, not about the constitution of obligation, and it cannot be attacked by the Euthyphro.
2. ⚠️ Do not attack the semantic version and claim to have refuted the theory. Almost no defender holds that "wrong" *means* "forbidden by God"; the metaphysical version is the target.
3. ⚠️ Do not omit the presuppositions. A script that states the four-step argument without naming the three assumptions has described the theory rather than examined it.

#### MINI RECAP

Command *constitutes* obligation. Four steps: authority, legislator, content, accountability. Three presuppositions: existence, knowability, authority-generates-reasons. Three strengths of "depends": motivational, metaphysical, semantic — and the middle one is the thesis.

---

### SESSION 3 — THE EUTHYPHRO DILEMMA: BOTH HORNS, AND THE PRICE OF EACH

#### DEFINITION

**Plain-language definition.** Plato asks: do the gods love holy things *because* they are holy, or are holy things holy *because* the gods love them? Transposed to morality: is an act good because God commands it, or does God command it because it is good?

**Technical definition.** **[FACT]** The dilemma, stated at *Euthyphro* 10a, is: *is the pious loved by the gods because it is pious, or pious because it is loved?* It is a **destructive dilemma** aimed at the constitutive claim. **Horn 1** — good *because* commanded — makes morality **arbitrary**: God could have commanded cruelty and cruelty would then be good. **Horn 2** — commanded *because* good — concedes that **goodness is independent of God**, who recognises a standard he did not invent. Horn 2 is the classical refutation of strong divine command theory.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> The Euthyphro is not an argument against God; it is an argument against a *theory* of obligation, and its force is that it offers the theist a choice between a morality he would not want and a God he did not intend.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**Euthyphro 10a · destructive dilemma · arbitrariness horn · independence horn · voluntarism · anything-whatever objection · vacuity objection · conceptual independence of goodness**

**How to use them:** name the location (*Euthyphro* 10a) and state **both** horns in the same sentence. A script that gives one horn has given half a dilemma, which is not a dilemma; and an examiner reads the second horn as the test of whether the candidate understands what a dilemma is.

#### VISUAL — THE FORK, AND WHAT IS LOST ON EACH SIDE

```text
                  IS X GOOD BECAUSE GOD COMMANDS IT?
                                 │
              ┌──────────────────┴───────────────────┐
              ▼                                      ▼
      HORN 1: YES                             HORN 2: NO
      command CONSTITUTES goodness            God commands it BECAUSE
                                              it is already good
              │                                      │
              ▼                                      ▼
      ARBITRARINESS                          INDEPENDENCE
      · God could have commanded cruelty     · goodness is a standard God
        and cruelty would be good              RECOGNISES, not one He invents
      · "God is good" becomes empty          · morality is AUTONOMOUS of God
        ("God does what God commands")       · divine command theory is FALSE
      · morality loses its necessity           as a theory of GROUNDING
              │                                      │
              ▼                                      ▼
      COST: the content of morality          COST: the theory the theist
      becomes contingent on a will           set out to defend
              └──────────────┬───────────────────────┘
                             ▼
              THE ESCAPE ROUTES (Sessions 4 and 6)
              · goodness = the divine NATURE, not a decree     (Adams)
              · God is the supreme STANDARD, not a conformer   (Alston)
              · God's MOTIVES, not commands, ground value      (Zagzebski)
              · goods are knowable by reason within an order
                grounded in divine reason                       (natural law)
```

*Follow either branch downward and read the cost at the bottom. The escape routes at the foot of the diagram are the content of the next two sessions — and each of them buys relief on one horn by conceding something on the other.*

#### TEACHING

**[FACT] The first horn and the arbitrariness objection.** If the command constitutes goodness, then had God commanded cruelty, cruelty would be good. Nothing internal to the theory rules that out, because on this horn there is no standard prior to the command against which a command could be assessed.

**[FACT] The vacuity consequence.** ⚠️ There is a second, sharper consequence of the first horn that stronger scripts add: the sentence "God is good" ceases to say anything substantive. If goodness just is what God commands, then "God is good" reduces to "God does what God commands" — a tautology, not the central affirmation of theistic religion. The first horn therefore threatens not only morality but the doctrine of divine goodness itself.

**[FACT] The second horn and the independence objection.** If God commands acts because they are already good, goodness is not constituted by command. God then recognises a standard he did not invent, and morality is autonomous of God at the level of grounding — which is the classical refutation of the strong theory.

**[INFERENCE] Why the dilemma is not a proof of atheism.** It is worth one sentence in the answer: the dilemma is entirely compatible with theism. Horn 2 is a perfectly comfortable position for a theist who is not a divine command theorist — the natural-law tradition sits there deliberately. What the dilemma refutes is a *theory of grounding*, not a religion.

**[FACT] What the dilemma leaves standing.** The Euthyphro shows morality **cannot be wholly reducible to** divine command, and therefore that morality has autonomy at the level of grounding. It leaves entirely untouched the claims that religion powerfully motivates and sustains moral life, and that morality in turn purifies religion — which is why the balanced conclusion of the whole clause is the **interaction** view rather than strict dependence.

**Objection.** The dilemma is a false one. God's nature is necessarily good, so the option "God commands cruelty" was never live; the fork presupposes a divine will floating free of a divine nature.
**Reply.** **[INFERENCE]** This is the right objection and it is the road to Session 4 — but notice what it does. It escapes the first horn by grounding goodness in the divine *nature* rather than the divine *will*. That is a genuine repair, and it is also a partial concession: goodness is now prior to command. The dilemma has been blocked at the level of *arbitrariness* while being conceded at the level of *conceptual priority*.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH

> **Claim:** The Euthyphro dilemma refutes strong divine command theory by forcing a choice between an arbitrary morality and a morality independent of God.
> **Named evidence:** Plato's question at *Euthyphro* 10a — whether the pious is loved because it is pious or pious because it is loved — yields the arbitrariness horn, on which cruelty would have been good had it been commanded, and the independence horn, on which God recognises a standard he did not create.
> **Analysis:** The first horn additionally empties the affirmation of divine goodness, since "God is good" collapses into "God commands what God commands"; the second horn concedes precisely the grounding claim the theory was built to secure.
> **Qualification:** The dilemma refutes a theory of grounding, not theism: a theist may occupy the second horn comfortably, as the natural-law tradition does, and the modified theory blocks the first horn at the price of conceding that goodness is conceptually prior to command.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Do not reduce the Euthyphro to "God versus morality". Specify **both** horns; the dilemma is the fork, not one of its prongs.
2. ⚠️ Do not present the dilemma as an argument against God's existence. It is an argument against a theory of moral grounding and is fully available to a theist.
3. ⚠️ Do not stop at arbitrariness. The vacuity consequence — that "God is good" becomes tautologous on the first horn — is what separates a good script from an average one.

#### MINI RECAP

*Euthyphro* 10a. Horn 1: arbitrary morality plus vacuous divine goodness. Horn 2: goodness independent of God. It kills a theory of grounding, not a religion — and every later repair pays on one horn to be relieved on the other.

---

### SESSION 4 — MODIFIED DIVINE COMMAND: ADAMS, ALSTON, ZAGZEBSKI AND THE RESIDUE

#### DEFINITION

**Plain-language definition.** The modified theory keeps God at the centre of obligation but stops the arbitrariness objection by saying that God's commands cannot be arbitrary, because they flow from a nature that is essentially loving.

**Technical definition.** **[FACT]** **Robert Merrihew Adams** (*Finite and Infinite Goods*, 1999) reformulates divine command theory so that moral **obligation** consists in the commands of a **loving God**, while **goodness** is grounded in resemblance to God's own nature rather than in commands. **William Alston's** parallel "standard" reply holds that God is the **supreme standard** of goodness rather than a conformer to an external one — as the standard metre bar was the standard of length without there being some further length it had to match — so there is neither a vicious regress nor arbitrariness. **Linda Zagzebski's** **divine motivation theory** is the virtue-theoretic variant: God's *motives*, not God's commands, are the metaphysical ground of moral value.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> The modified theory is the most interesting move in the whole clause because it wins and loses in the same sentence: it secures non-arbitrariness by grounding commands in a loving nature, and in doing so it concedes that we must already understand "loving" morally before the theory can get started.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**Robert Merrihew Adams · *Finite and Infinite Goods* · obligation from a loving God's commands · goodness as resemblance to the divine nature · William Alston · supreme standard · standard-metre analogy · Linda Zagzebski · divine motivation theory · residual conceptual independence**

**How to use them:** name Adams and state the *split* — obligation from commands, goodness from resemblance to nature. Then add the residual objection in the same paragraph. Stating the repair without the residue reads as advocacy; stating both reads as evaluation, and evaluation is what the marks are for.

#### VISUAL — THE REPAIR, AND WHAT IT COSTS

```text
   UNMODIFIED THEORY                    MODIFIED THEORY (Adams)
   ─────────────────                    ───────────────────────
   OBLIGATION ← command                 OBLIGATION ← command of an
                                                     ESSENTIALLY LOVING God
   GOODNESS   ← command                 GOODNESS   ← RESEMBLANCE to the
                                                     divine nature
        │                                     │
        ▼                                     ▼
   Horn 1 bites:                        Horn 1 BLOCKED:
   cruelty could                        a loving nature cannot
   have been good                       issue a cruel command
                                              │
                                              ▼
                                     ┌────────────────────────┐
                                     │  RESIDUAL OBJECTION    │
                                     │  "loving" and "good"   │
                                     │  are moral concepts we │
                                     │  must ALREADY grasp    │
                                     │  independently — else  │
                                     │  the claim is empty.   │
                                     └────────────────────────┘
                                              │
                                              ▼
                             NON-ARBITRARINESS secured
                             CONCEPTUAL INDEPENDENCE conceded

   ALSTON'S PARALLEL REPAIR              ZAGZEBSKI'S VARIANT
   God = the SUPREME STANDARD,           God's MOTIVES, not God's
   not a conformer to a further one      commands, ground moral value
   (as the standard metre bar was        --> shifts the theory from
   the standard of length without            command-ethics to
   matching some prior length)               virtue-ethics
   --> no regress, no arbitrariness
```

*The left column is what the Euthyphro destroys; the right column is what survives it, together with the bill. Write both sides: the repair is only worth marks if its price is stated.*

#### TEACHING

**[FACT] Adams's split.** The decisive move is to *divide the question*. Obligation and goodness are given different grounds: obligation is generated by the commands of a God who is essentially loving; goodness is grounded in resemblance to that God's nature. Once split, the first horn cannot bite, because a nature that is essentially loving cannot issue a command to cruelty. The possibility the arbitrariness objection depends on is closed off by the modal structure of the divine nature rather than by a promise about divine behaviour.

**[FACT] Alston's standard reply.** Alston attacks the assumed regress. If God is the supreme standard of goodness, then asking "but is God good by some further standard?" is like asking whether the standard metre bar is really a metre long: the question misconstrues what a standard is. There is therefore no external standard God conforms to, and no arbitrariness either.

**[FACT] Zagzebski's variant.** Divine motivation theory relocates the ground from commands to *motives*: what is metaphysically basic is God's motivational states, and human virtues are good in so far as they exemplify them. This converts a command-ethic into a virtue-ethic and thereby sidesteps the machinery of legislation altogether.

**[FACT] The residual objection — state it, it is what distinguishes an evaluative answer.** ⚠️ Calling the divine nature "loving" or "good" uses a moral concept whose content we must already grasp independently, otherwise the claim is empty. The modified theory therefore secures **non-arbitrariness** at the cost of conceding **conceptual independence** to goodness.

**[INFERENCE] How to grade the repair honestly.** The right verdict is neither "the modified theory refutes the Euthyphro" nor "the modified theory fails". It is that the modified theory **relocates** the dilemma: it moves the question from "why is this command binding?" to "in what does the goodness of the divine nature consist?" — and that relocation is a genuine gain in the first horn and a genuine concession in the second. Say that, and the examiner has read an evaluation.

**Objection.** The residual objection proves too much. Every foundational theory must use concepts we already grasp; a Kantian must already grasp "rational", a utilitarian "welfare". If that is fatal to Adams it is fatal to everyone.
**Reply.** **[INFERENCE]** A fair and strong reply that should be included. The response is that the parity is incomplete: the divine command theorist's distinctive claim was that moral concepts are *grounded* in God, whereas the Kantian never claimed that "rational" was grounded in the categorical imperative. It is the direction of grounding, not the mere use of a prior concept, that the residue embarrasses.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH

> **Claim:** Modified divine command theory blocks the arbitrariness horn of the Euthyphro but does so by conceding the conceptual priority of goodness.
> **Named evidence:** Adams, in *Finite and Infinite Goods*, grounds obligation in the commands of an essentially loving God and goodness in resemblance to the divine nature; Alston answers the regress by treating God as the supreme standard, on the model of the standard metre bar; Zagzebski relocates the ground from commands to divine motives.
> **Analysis:** The split works: a necessarily loving nature cannot command cruelty, so the counterexample the first horn requires is unavailable — but the repair depends on our recognising "loving" as good before the theory is applied, which is exactly the independence the second horn asserts.
> **Qualification:** The honest verdict is relocation rather than refutation, since the dilemma reappears one level up as the question of what the goodness of the divine nature consists in — and a defender may fairly reply that every foundational theory borrows some concept it does not itself ground.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Do not present modified divine command theory as defeating the Euthyphro outright. It blocks arbitrariness but concedes that "loving" and "good" are concepts we grasp independently.
2. ⚠️ Do not attribute the standard-metre analogy to Adams. It is Alston's reply; Adams's contribution is the obligation/goodness split.
3. ⚠️ Do not confuse divine *motivation* theory with divine *command* theory. Zagzebski grounds value in God's motives, which makes it a virtue theory, not a legislative one.

#### MINI RECAP

Adams splits obligation (command of a loving God) from goodness (resemblance to divine nature). Alston: supreme standard, no regress. Zagzebski: motives, not commands. Residue: "loving" is a moral concept we already possess — non-arbitrariness bought with conceptual independence.

---

### SESSION 5 — KANTIAN AUTONOMY: OBLIGATION WITHOUT A COMMANDER

#### DEFINITION

**Plain-language definition.** Kant says the moral law binds you because *you*, as a rational being, legislate it — not because someone with more power than you issued it. Being moral out of fear of punishment is not being moral at all; it is being prudent.

**Technical definition.** **[FACT]** Moral obligation arises from **rational self-legislation** under a universally valid law, not from heteronomous reward, punishment or command. Morality rests on reason and autonomy — the categorical imperative — and not on God's command. Religion is *related* to morality, but God, freedom and immortality function as **practical postulates** rather than as the source of the moral law.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Kant does not remove God from morality; he removes God from the *foundation* of morality — and the difference matters, because on Kant's account a God who grounded obligation by commanding it would have destroyed the very thing he was grounding.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**autonomy · heteronomy · rational self-legislation · categorical imperative · universal law · humanity as an end · kingdom of ends · practical postulates · action from duty · moral worth · formalism objection**

**How to use them:** the sentence that earns the mark is "autonomy is not doing as one pleases; it is obedience to a law one gives oneself as a rational being". Write it early, because the commonest misreading in this clause is to equate autonomy with moral subjectivism, and pre-empting that misreading is itself worth marks.

#### VISUAL — THE FOUR-STEP ARGUMENT, AND THE AUTONOMY / HETERONOMY FORK

```text
   KANT'S ARGUMENT (four steps)
   1  Moral worth requires action FROM DUTY, not merely in accordance with it.
   2  Contingent DESIRE cannot yield NECESSITY — and obligation is necessary.
   3  Rational agents therefore test MAXIMS for universal law.
   4  Autonomy = obedience to reason ONE SHARES AS RATIONAL, i.e. self-legislation.

   THE FORK THAT DECIDES THE CLAUSE
   ┌───────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┐
   │ AUTONOMY                      │ HETERONOMY                           │
   │ law given by reason to itself │ law given by ANOTHER will, or by     │
   │                               │ desire, reward, fear or punishment   │
   ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
   │ obligation is NECESSARY and   │ obligation becomes CONDITIONAL:      │
   │ unconditional                 │ "if you want the reward, then..."    │
   ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
   │ MORAL WORTH preserved         │ moral worth LOST — the act is prudent│
   └───────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┘
                     ↓                              ↓
       DIVINE COMMAND, if it works through          THE CANONICAL EXAMPLE
       sanction, falls on the RIGHT side of         a truthful act motivated only
       this fork — which is the deepest             by fear of divine punishment
       Kantian objection to it, deeper than         lacks the moral worth of the
       the Euthyphro                                same act done from duty

   WHAT IS *NOT* BEING SAID
   autonomy ≠ "each person decides what is right"    ← subjectivism, not Kant
   autonomy = self-legislation of a UNIVERSAL law shared by all rational agents
   God, freedom, immortality = PRACTICAL POSTULATES, not the SOURCE of the law
```

*The upper box is the argument; the fork is the discriminator; the bottom strip is the misreading that costs most candidates their marks in this clause.*

#### TEACHING

**[FACT] The argument.** (1) Moral worth requires action *from* duty rather than merely in conformity with it. (2) Contingent desire cannot yield the necessity that obligation has. (3) Rational agents therefore test their maxims for universal law. (4) Autonomy is obedience to a reason one shares as a rational being — which is why it is freedom rather than constraint.

**[FACT] The presupposition.** ⚠️ That practical reason can generate categorical norms at all. This is the assumption a divine command theorist denies, and naming it keeps the exchange between the two positions honest rather than rhetorical.

**[FACT] The distinction that must be made.** Autonomy is **not** doing whatever one wants. It is freedom *under a self-given universal rational law*. The opposite of autonomy is not restraint but **heteronomy**: a law given by another will or by desire.

**[FACT] The canonical example.** A truthful act motivated only by fear of divine punishment lacks the same autonomous moral worth as the same act performed from duty. This is the sharpest illustration of why a sanction-based religious morality is, for Kant, a defective morality rather than a strong one.

**[FACT] The relation to religion.** God, freedom and immortality enter Kant's practical philosophy as **postulates** — what practical reason must presuppose for the moral life to be coherent — and not as the ground of obligation. Religion is thereby *related* to morality without being its foundation. This is the exact sentence that answers a "must normative principles reference God?" stem.

**[FACT] The standing objection: formalism.** ⚠️ Formal universalisation may underdetermine content — a sufficiently cleverly described maxim can be universalised. **Reply:** Kantians add the **humanity formula** (treat humanity always also as an end, never merely as a means) and the **kingdom of ends**, which restore substantive content to the test.

**[INFERENCE] The motivation gap, stated in advance.** Kant's account is strongest exactly where divine command theory is weakest — grounding — and weakest exactly where divine command theory is strongest: it explains why the moral law binds, not why *this* agent will act on it when inclination pulls hard. Session 14 is built on that gap; naming it here shows the examiner you know it is coming.

**Objection.** If God is only a postulate, Kant has made religion morally idle: a decoration on an already complete ethics.
**Reply.** **[INFERENCE]** Not idle, but relocated. On Kant's account the postulates answer a real question that morality raises and cannot answer from its own resources — the rational hope that virtue and happiness are not permanently divorced. Religion is therefore not the *ground* of the moral law but the rational completion of the moral outlook, which is a smaller role than the theist wanted and a larger one than "decoration".

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH

> **Claim:** Kant grounds moral obligation in rational autonomy, so normative principles need not refer to God in order to bind.
> **Named evidence:** In the *Groundwork*, moral worth requires action from duty; contingent desire cannot yield necessity; maxims are therefore tested for universal law; and God, freedom and immortality enter only as practical postulates rather than as the source of the law.
> **Analysis:** Because heteronomy — a law given by another will, or by reward and fear — destroys the unconditional character of obligation, a divine command that operates through sanction is not a stronger foundation for morality but a defective one, which is a deeper objection than the Euthyphro.
> **Qualification:** The account is vulnerable to the formalism charge, answered by the humanity and kingdom-of-ends formulations, and it is admittedly stronger on why the law binds than on why a reluctant agent will obey it — which is where the religious answer re-enters.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Do not equate autonomy with moral subjectivism. Kantian autonomy is self-legislation of a *universal* law, and the misreading inverts the doctrine.
2. ⚠️ Do not say Kant "removes God from morality". God, freedom and immortality remain as practical postulates; what is removed is God's role as the *ground* of obligation.
3. ⚠️ Do not ignore the formalism objection. A script that presents the categorical imperative without the underdetermination worry and the humanity-formula reply has not examined the position.

#### MINI RECAP

Duty, not inclination; necessity, not desire; universalisation; self-legislation. Autonomy versus heteronomy is the fork, and sanction-based religion falls on the wrong side of it. God as postulate, not ground. Formalism answered by humanity and the kingdom of ends.

---

### SESSION 6 — NATURAL LAW: THE MEDIATING POSITION BETWEEN COMMAND AND AUTONOMY

#### DEFINITION

**Plain-language definition.** Natural law says God did not have to *tell* us what is right, because he made a world in which certain things really are good for creatures like us, and reason can find that out.

**Technical definition.** **[FACT]** Moral norms are **rationally accessible participations in an objective order** grounded ultimately in divine reason, not in arbitrary decrees. Human goods and inclinations disclose basic ends; practical reason directs conduct toward them; divine grounding explains why that order is objective rather than conventional.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Natural law is the position the Euthyphro was always going to leave standing: it accepts the second horn deliberately, holding that God grounds the order of goods without issuing a decree for each duty — which is why it is the most durable theistic ethics rather than the most defensive one.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**natural law · objective order of goods · practical reason · basic goods · participation · theistic moral realism · rationally accessible without revelation · nature-to-norm problem · naturalistic fallacy · goods as reasons**

**How to use them:** the mediating claim is the mark-earner: "God grounds the order, but reason knows much of it without revelation." That one sentence positions natural law correctly against both rivals and shows you know the map rather than two points on it.

#### VISUAL — WHERE NATURAL LAW SITS

```text
   STRONG DIVINE COMMAND ────── NATURAL LAW ────── KANTIAN AUTONOMY
   obligation = decree            order of goods     obligation = rational
                                  grounded in            self-legislation
                                  divine reason,
                                  KNOWN by reason
   ├─ God supplies CONTENT        ├─ God supplies      ├─ reason supplies
   │  by commanding               │  the ORDER;        │  both order and
   │                              │  reason supplies   │  content
   │                              │  the ACCESS        │
   ├─ Euthyphro horn 1 bites      ├─ occupies horn 2   ├─ Euthyphro does not
   │                              │  DELIBERATELY      │  arise
   └─ revelation needed for       └─ revelation not    └─ revelation not
      moral knowledge                needed for much      needed at all
                                     of moral knowledge

   THE ARGUMENT                            THE STANDING OBJECTION
   1 human goods and inclinations          "nature-to-norm": from what humans
     disclose basic ENDS                    ARE, nothing follows about what
   2 practical reason DIRECTS conduct       they OUGHT to do — the naturalistic
     toward them                            fallacy
   3 divine grounding explains their                     ↓
     OBJECTIVE order                        REPLY: practical reason grasps goods
                                            AS REASONS, not as bare biological
   CANONICAL EXAMPLE                        facts; the premise is normative from
   preservation of life and social          the start, not smuggled in from
   cooperation are treated as BASIC         biology
   GOODS — not good merely because
   a text commands them
```

*Read left to right for the map and top to bottom in the right-hand column for the objection that always comes. The mediating position is defined by what it takes from each neighbour: objectivity from the theist, accessibility from the rationalist.*

#### TEACHING

**[FACT] The doctrine.** Moral norms are rationally accessible participations in an objective order that is grounded ultimately in divine reason rather than in decree. The word doing the work is *participation*: human practical reason does not receive the order from outside, it shares in it.

**[FACT] The argument.** Human goods and inclinations disclose basic ends; practical reason directs conduct toward those ends; divine grounding explains why the order of ends is objective rather than a human construction.

**[FACT] The presupposition.** ⚠️ That human nature has intelligible normative ends. This is exactly what a modern anti-teleological view of nature denies, and it should be named rather than assumed.

**[FACT] The mediating distinction.** Natural law mediates between command theory and secular autonomy: **God grounds the order, but reason knows much of it without revelation.** That single clause is the position's whole strategic value in an answer.

**[FACT] The canonical example.** Preservation of life and social cooperation are treated as basic goods — good in themselves for beings of our kind — not good merely because a text commands them. The example matters because it shows the theory delivering content without a decree.

**[FACT] The objection and reply.** ⚠️ The move from nature to norm risks the naturalistic fallacy. **Reply:** practical reason grasps goods *as reasons*, not as bare biological facts; the starting point is already normative, so nothing is being illegitimately derived from a purely descriptive premise.

**[INFERENCE] Why this session changes the shape of an answer.** Most scripts run a two-position debate: divine command versus Kant. Introducing natural law converts it into a three-position debate in which the theist is no longer forced onto the arbitrariness horn. That is a structural gain: it lets you refute strong divine command theory *without* concluding that theism has nothing to say about grounding — which is a much more defensible verdict than the usual one.

**Objection.** Natural law is divine command theory with better manners: God still grounds morality, so the Euthyphro simply reappears as "is the order good because God grounds it, or does God ground it because it is good?"
**Reply.** **[INFERENCE]** The objection is well aimed but the reply is available. On the natural-law account the order is grounded in divine *reason* rather than divine *will*, and the arbitrariness horn was generated by will. A God whose reason is identical with his nature does not choose the order in the way a legislator chooses a statute, so there is no moment at which the order could have been otherwise. The dilemma is defused rather than answered — which is, honestly, what the modified theory also achieves.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH

> **Claim:** Natural law occupies the middle ground: it grounds morality theistically without making obligation depend on a decree, and it makes moral knowledge available to reason.
> **Named evidence:** Basic goods such as the preservation of life and social cooperation are treated as objective ends disclosed by human inclination and directed by practical reason, with divine reason — not divine will — explaining the objectivity of the order.
> **Analysis:** Because the ground is reason rather than will, the arbitrariness horn cannot be generated, and because the access is rational rather than revelatory, the epistemic dependence claim is dropped by a theist rather than by a critic.
> **Qualification:** The position stands or falls on the nature-to-norm inference, and the reply that practical reason grasps goods as reasons rather than as biological facts is a defensible answer rather than a knock-down one.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Do not conflate natural law with divine command theory. Natural law grounds morality in an *order* known by reason; command theory grounds it in a *decree* known by revelation.
2. ⚠️ Do not present natural law as a purely secular ethics either. Divine reason is doing real grounding work; removing it turns the theory into ordinary moral realism.
3. ⚠️ Do not ignore the naturalistic-fallacy objection. Stating it and giving the goods-as-reasons reply is worth more than three additional sentences of exposition.

#### MINI RECAP

Order, not decree. Reason, not revelation, for access. Basic goods: life, cooperation. Objection: nature-to-norm. Reply: goods are grasped as reasons. Strategic value: it makes the debate three-cornered and stops the theist being trapped on horn 1.

---

### SESSION 7 — SECULAR ETHICS: FOUR GROUNDINGS AND THE OBJECTION EACH INCURS

#### DEFINITION

**Plain-language definition.** Secular ethics is the claim that you can build a complete, demanding morality without any religious premise at all — and it is not one theory but a family of them.

**Technical definition.** **[FACT]** Utilitarianism, contractarianism, virtue ethics and humanism all ground morality without theism: respectively in consequences and welfare, in mutually advantageous agreement, in character and flourishing, and in human dignity and solidarity. Each is a *grounding* claim, and each carries a well-known standing difficulty.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> The strength of secular ethics is not that any one of its theories is unanswerable but that there are several of them, developed independently and disagreeing with one another — because a dependence thesis is refuted not by one alternative but by the existence of a field.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**utilitarianism · welfare and impartial maximisation · contractarianism · mutual advantage · virtue ethics · eudaimonism · humanism · dignity and solidarity · demandingness objection · circularity objection · thick-nature dependence · grounding of obligation**

**How to use them:** never write "secular ethics" as a single lump. Name at least two families and the objection each incurs. The examiner is testing whether you know that the alternatives disagree with each other, because that is what shows the field is real rather than a placeholder for "not religion".

#### VISUAL — FOUR GROUNDINGS, FOUR BILLS

```text
  ┌────────────────┬───────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │ FAMILY         │ GROUND OF OBLIGATION      │ STANDING OBJECTION       │
  ├────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Utilitarian    │ consequences and welfare;  │ rights and DEMANDINGNESS │
  │                │ impartial maximisation     │ — it can require too much│
  │                │                            │ and protect too little   │
  ├────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Contractarian  │ mutual advantage; rational │ the perfect KNAVE — one  │
  │                │ agreement among equals     │ who breaks the agreement │
  │                │                            │ undetected is unanswered │
  ├────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Virtue /       │ character and human        │ presupposes a THICK      │
  │ eudaimonist    │ flourishing                │ account of human nature  │
  │                │                            │ the sceptic can decline  │
  ├────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Humanist       │ dignity, solidarity and    │ asked to explain the     │
  │                │ the worth of persons       │ SOURCE of that worth     │
  │                │                            │ without a metaphysics    │
  └────────────────┴───────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘
          ↓                                              ↓
   THE COMMON STRENGTH                          THE COMMON CHALLENGE
   none of the four needs a                     each must explain the
   religious premise at any                     UNCONDITIONAL character
   point in its derivation                      of obligation from
                                                resources that look
                                                conditional
```

*Read the middle column for what each theory says and the right column for what it owes. The bottom strip is the fair summary: the field succeeds at independence and is still working at unconditionality.*

#### TEACHING

**[FACT] The claim.** Utilitarianism, contractarianism, virtue ethics and humanism all ground morality without theism. This is a *plural* fact, and its plurality is the argument: a necessity claim is defeated by the existence of working alternatives, and it is defeated more decisively by several alternatives that reached independence by different routes.

**[FACT] Utilitarian grounding, and its bill.** Obligation is grounded in the impartial maximisation of welfare. The standing objections are the protection of rights, which impartial maximisation can override, and demandingness, since a fully impartial calculus can require more of an agent than moral common sense allows.

**[FACT] Contractarian grounding, and its bill.** Obligation is grounded in mutually advantageous agreement among rational parties. The standing objection is the one that returns in Session 15: the agent who can break the agreement without detection has not yet been given a reason not to.

**[FACT] Virtue and eudaimonist grounding, and its bill.** Being moral is partly *constitutive* of flourishing rather than a cost paid out of it. The standing objection is that this presupposes a thick account of human nature, which a sceptic may simply decline to share.

**[FACT] Humanist grounding, and its bill.** Obligation is grounded in the dignity and worth of persons and in human solidarity. The standing objection is a demand for the *source* of that worth once a metaphysical or theological account of it has been refused.

**[FACT] The general theistic objection: the motivation gap.** ⚠️ Secular reason may identify duty and yet fail to motivate. **Reply:** moral motivation can arise from empathy, identity and respect; religion is *one* source of motivation, not a logically necessary one. This is the exchange in its cleanest form, and both halves belong in the answer.

**[INFERENCE] What the field does and does not establish.** It establishes the falsity of the *necessity* claim: obligation can be grounded without theistic premises, and has been, repeatedly. It does not establish that any one secular grounding is correct, nor that the theistic groundings are incoherent. An answer that claims the second has overshot the evidence and will be marked down for it.

**Objection.** These theories all quietly live off religious capital: dignity, equality and impartial concern entered Western moral thought through religious traditions, so secular ethics is parasitic rather than independent.
**Reply.** **[INFERENCE]** The historical premise deserves a genuine concession — moral vocabularies do have religious histories, and Session 8 documents the influence. But a **genetic** point cannot settle a **justificatory** one. Where a norm came from is a different question from what now justifies it, and the availability of non-theistic *derivations* of impartial concern — including, decisively, ancient Indian ones that never had the alleged religious capital in the first place — is what answers the objection rather than denying its history.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH

> **Claim:** Secular ethics defeats the necessity claim not by producing one unanswerable theory but by producing a plural, mutually disagreeing field of groundings.
> **Named evidence:** Utilitarianism grounds obligation in impartial welfare, contractarianism in mutual advantage, virtue ethics in flourishing, and humanism in dignity and solidarity — with demandingness, the undetected defector, thick human nature and the source of worth as their respective standing objections.
> **Analysis:** Because the four reach independence by different routes and criticise one another, their existence is evidence about the possibility of non-theistic grounding rather than advocacy of any single theory.
> **Qualification:** The field establishes that theism is not necessary for grounding, not that it is false or superfluous, and the honest concession is that the motivation gap identified by the theist is real even where the grounding gap is not.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Do not write "secular ethics" as though it were one theory. Naming at least two families and their objections is the difference between an average and a good script.
2. ⚠️ Do not treat the availability of secular grounding as a refutation of theistic ethics. It refutes a *necessity* claim only.
3. ⚠️ Do not answer the "religious capital" objection by denying the history. Concede the genealogy and separate it from justification; that is the philosophically correct move and it reads as confidence.

#### MINI RECAP

Four families, four grounds, four bills. Plurality is the argument. What is proved: theism is not necessary. What is not proved: that theism is wrong. The motivation gap survives the grounding argument and is taken up in Session 14.

---

### SESSION 8 — THE INTERACTION VIEW I: HOW RELIGION ACTUALLY INFLUENCES CONDUCT

#### DEFINITION

**Plain-language definition.** Even if religion does not *make* things right, it plainly changes how people behave: it gives reasons to care, people to imitate, a community that notices, and a story in which being good makes sense.

**Technical definition.** **[FACT]** Religion can **motivate, socialise and symbolically deepen** morality, but the influence is **contingent** and may also legitimise exclusion. The mechanisms are narrative exemplars, ritual formation, community sanction, hope and repentance, and a worldview that sustains moral commitment over time. Causal influence does **not** establish logical dependence or moral truth.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> That religion influences moral behaviour is not in dispute; what is in dispute is what follows from it — and the correct answer is that a causal claim, however well supported, cannot cross the gap into a claim about what makes conduct right.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**motivation · socialisation · narrative exemplars · ritual formation · community sanction · hope and repentance · sustaining worldview · contingent influence · causal influence is not logical dependence · legitimation of exclusion**

**How to use them:** the 2023 stem asks for **two** deliverables — the influence *and* the interaction model. Name the mechanisms of influence in one paragraph, then state the two-way model in the next. A script that gives only the first has answered half the question and will be capped accordingly.

#### VISUAL — THE FIVE MECHANISMS, AND THE WALL THEY CANNOT CROSS

```text
   HOW RELIGION MOVES CONDUCT — five mechanisms
   ┌──────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │ 1 EXEMPLARS      │ narrative models of a life worth imitating        │
   │ 2 RITUAL         │ repeated practice forms habit and disposition     │
   │ 3 COMMUNITY      │ visibility, belonging, approval and sanction      │
   │ 4 HOPE/REPENTANCE│ failure is survivable, so effort is renewable     │
   │ 5 WORLDVIEW      │ a frame in which moral effort is not absurd       │
   └──────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                              │
                              ▼
   ══════════════ THE WALL ══════════════════════════════════════════════
   CAUSAL INFLUENCE  ─X→  LOGICAL DEPENDENCE
   CAUSAL INFLUENCE  ─X→  MORAL TRUTH
   "religious participation correlates with charitable giving"
       ⇒ religion may CAUSALLY influence conduct
       ⇏ religion MAKES charity right
       ⇏ non-believers CANNOT KNOW charity is right
       ⇏ divine command theory survives the Euthyphro
   ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
                              │
                              ▼
   THE SYMMETRY THAT MUST BE STATED
   the same mechanisms that support care can SANCTION EXCLUSION:
   exemplars can be partisan · ritual can substitute for substance ·
   community sanction can police a boundary · hope can be promised
   selectively · a worldview can divide the world
   ⇒ the influence is CONTINGENT, not inherently moral or immoral
```

*The top box is the answer to "does religion influence moral behaviour?"; the wall is the answer to what follows from it; the bottom strip is what prevents the answer from becoming an advertisement.*

#### TEACHING

**[FACT] The five mechanisms.** Religion supplies **motivation** (reward and punishment, love of God, gratitude), **community and sanction**, **moral exemplars**, and a **worldview** that sustains moral commitment; narrative exemplars, ritual formation, community sanctions, hope and repentance can stabilise conduct over time in a way that isolated resolution does not.

**[FACT] The canonical example.** A vow of non-violence may be strengthened by religious discipline, while its moral defensibility still requires reasons accessible beyond the community. The example is precisely constructed: it concedes the psychological reality of the strengthening and denies that the strengthening does any justificatory work.

**[FACT] The causal caution.** ⚠️ Religious narratives and institutions can support care *or* sanction exclusion. This contingent influence does not establish that religion is inherently moral or inherently immoral. The symmetry is what makes the caution honest rather than defensive: the same five mechanisms run in both directions.

**[FACT] The presupposition to state.** ⚠️ Belief and practice meaningfully shape agency but do not mechanically determine it. Without that qualification the claim becomes a determinism no one holds; with it, the claim is modest and defensible.

**[FACT] What behavioural evidence establishes.** ⚠️ At most a contingent influence, and even that may require controls for community, selection and orientation. It cannot show what makes an act right, who can know its rightness, or whether divine command theory survives the Euthyphro. A correlation between participation and giving is evidence about people, not about obligation.

**[INFERENCE] The right shape for a 2023-type answer.** Deliverable one: yes, religion influences moral behaviour, and here are the five mechanisms. Deliverable two: the relation is *interactive*, because morality also acts on religion — which is the content of Session 9. The interaction model is the answer to the second half of the stem, and forgetting it is the standard way to lose marks on an otherwise competent script.

**Objection.** If the influence is contingent and can run either way, then citing it proves nothing at all, and the whole interaction view is empty.
**Reply.** **[INFERENCE]** Contingency is not emptiness. A contingent influence is still a real influence, and it is *assessable*: traditions can be compared by how strongly they build internal criticism and moral universalism into their formation of agents. What contingency rules out is the *inference* from influence to inherent moral quality — not the study of the influence itself.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH

> **Claim:** Religion demonstrably influences moral behaviour through identifiable mechanisms, but the influence is contingent and carries no implication about moral truth.
> **Named evidence:** Narrative exemplars, ritual formation, community sanction, hope and repentance, and a sustaining worldview are the mechanisms; the vow of non-violence strengthened by religious discipline is the canonical case, since the strengthening is real while the justification still requires publicly accessible reasons.
> **Analysis:** Because the same mechanisms can sanction exclusion as readily as they support care, no inference runs from observed influence to the inherent moral quality of religion, and none runs from influence to the constitution of obligation.
> **Qualification:** Contingency does not make the influence trivial: traditions remain comparable by how far they build internal criticism and moral universalism into the formation they perform.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Do not infer logical dependence from sociological influence. This trap recurs here in its most tempting form, because the influence really is well attested.
2. ⚠️ Do not answer a 2023-type stem with influence alone. The words "explain the interactive relation" are a second deliverable and are marked separately.
3. ⚠️ Do not present the influence as uniformly benign. Stating the exclusionary possibility in the same paragraph is what makes the account evidential rather than promotional.

#### MINI RECAP

Five mechanisms: exemplars, ritual, community, hope, worldview. One wall: causal influence never crosses into logical dependence or moral truth. One symmetry: the same mechanisms can exclude. Two deliverables in the printed stem — influence *and* interaction.

---

### SESSION 9 — THE INTERACTION VIEW II: MORALITY CRITICISES RELIGION

#### DEFINITION

**Plain-language definition.** The traffic runs both ways. Moral conscience judges religious practice — and it has done so most powerfully from *inside* religious traditions, not only from outside them.

**Technical definition.** **[FACT]** Moral conscience **critiques** religious practice — prophets against ritualism, reform movements against caste and *satī* — and a religion is judged by its **ethical fruits**. Religious moralities may conflict over duties, authority, exceptions and the interpretation of texts, so no inference runs from "religion supports morality" to "all religions share one moral code". Any claimed command remains answerable to **identification, interpretation, consistency, proportionality, human dignity and the moral status of the authority invoked**.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> The decisive evidence that morality is not simply derived from religion is that religions are themselves reformed by moral argument — and reformed most often by their own adherents, using moral concepts they did not suspend at the door.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**moral criticism of religion · prophetic critique · reform movements · ethical fruits · plural religious moralities · six tests of a claimed command · identification · interpretation · consistency · proportionality · human dignity · moral status of the authority · internal critique**

**How to use them:** the six tests are the highest-value list in this session. Write them as a checklist applied to a *claimed* command, and the answer immediately looks like philosophy of religion rather than commentary.

#### VISUAL — THE SIX TESTS, AND THE TWO-WAY ARROW

```text
        ┌───────────── RELIGION ─────────────┐
        │   motivates · forms · sustains     │
        │   supplies exemplars, community    │
        └────────────────┬───────────────────┘
                 influence ↓        ↑ criticism
        ┌────────────────┴───────────────────┐
        │            MORALITY                │
        │   judges practice · tests claims   │
        │   refuses sacred exemption         │
        └────────────────────────────────────┘

   SIX TESTS ANY CLAIMED COMMAND MUST PASS
   ┌───┬──────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │ 1 │ IDENTIFICATION   │ is this really a command, and whose?          │
   │ 2 │ INTERPRETATION   │ does the text/tradition bear this reading?     │
   │ 3 │ CONSISTENCY      │ does it cohere with the tradition's own norms? │
   │ 4 │ PROPORTIONALITY  │ is what is demanded proportionate?             │
   │ 5 │ HUMAN DIGNITY    │ does it treat persons as ends?                 │
   │ 6 │ AUTHORITY        │ what is the moral status of who is invoking it?│
   └───┴──────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────┘
   ⇒ this is moral criticism WITHIN AND ACROSS traditions,
```
