```text
     NOT automatic secular hostility.

   PLURAL MORALITIES — the inference that fails
   "religion supports morality"  ─X→  "all religions share one moral code"
   religious moralities conflict over: duties · authority · exceptions ·
   the interpretation of texts
```

*The double arrow is the interaction model in one image. The six-row table is what turns "morality criticises religion" from a slogan into a method — and the bottom strip blocks the inference that most scripts make without noticing.*

#### TEACHING

**[FACT] Morality refines religion.** Moral conscience critiques religious practice. The named instances are the prophetic critique of ritualism and the reform movements directed against caste and *satī*. A religion is judged by its **ethical fruits** — a pragmatic test, and one whose force is that religious traditions themselves accept it.

**[FACT] Plural moralities.** Religious moralities may conflict over duties, authority, exceptions and the interpretation of texts. Therefore no inference is valid from "religion supports morality" to "all religions share one moral code". This blocks a lazy conclusion that otherwise slips into almost every script on the interaction view.

**[FACT] The six tests.** A claimed command remains answerable to identification, interpretation, consistency, proportionality, human dignity and the moral status of the authority invoked. ⚠️ The point that must be stated in the same breath: this is moral criticism **within and across** traditions, not automatic secular hostility. Reformers inside a tradition apply these tests constantly.

**[FACT] The immoral-religion objection and its reply.** ⚠️ A religion may preserve rituals while sanctioning injustice. **Reply:** if morality is partly *constitutive* of worthy religion, such practice is **internal corruption** rather than a counterexample to ethical religion. That reply is not a dodge: it is the same move a jurisprudence makes when it calls a corrupt court a failure of law rather than a counterexample to law.

**[INFERENCE] Why internal critique is the strongest evidence available.** External moral criticism of a religion can always be met with the charge that alien standards are being imposed. Internal criticism cannot. When a tradition's own prophets, reformers and jurists condemn a practice using the tradition's own vocabulary, the moral concept being deployed is doing work the tradition already recognises — which shows that moral judgement is not simply downstream of religious authority even *inside* religion.

**Objection.** If morality can overrule religious command, morality has been made the judge of revelation, which is arrogance: the finite judging the infinite.
**Reply.** **[INFERENCE]** The objection misdescribes what happens. Interpretation of a claimed command already uses moral concepts — to identify which reading is the faithful one, which authority is legitimate, which application is proportionate. The moral concepts are therefore *already inside* the interpretive act; they are not imported afterwards to sit in judgement. What is being judged is a human claim about a command, not the divine as such.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH

> **Claim:** The relation between religion and morality is two-way, because moral conscience criticises and reforms religious practice as systematically as religion motivates moral conduct.
> **Named evidence:** The prophetic critique of ritualism, and reform movements against caste and *satī*, are moral criticisms mounted largely from within traditions; and any claimed command remains answerable to identification, interpretation, consistency, proportionality, human dignity and the moral standing of whoever invokes it.
> **Analysis:** Because those six tests are applied by adherents using their own tradition's vocabulary, moral judgement is shown to operate inside religion rather than only against it, which refutes the picture of morality as simply downstream of religious authority.
> **Qualification:** Religious moralities also conflict with one another over duties, authority and interpretation, so the interaction view licenses no conclusion that religions converge on a single moral code, and the immoral-religion case is best read as internal corruption rather than as a counterexample to ethical religion.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Do not infer from "religion supports morality" that all religions share one moral code. They conflict over duties, authority, exceptions and interpretation.
2. ⚠️ Do not present moral criticism of religion as inherently secular. The strongest instances are internal, and saying so is both accurate and more persuasive.
3. ⚠️ Do not deploy the internal-corruption reply as a blanket defence. It works only if morality is genuinely treated as constitutive of worthy religion — which is a substantive commitment, not a rhetorical escape.

#### MINI RECAP

Two-way arrow. Prophets, reformers, ethical fruits. Six tests of a claimed command. Plural moralities block the convergence inference. Immoral religion is internal corruption *if* morality is constitutive of worthy religion.

---

### SESSION 10 — INDIAN GROUNDS I: *DHARMA*, COSMIC ORDER (*ṚTA*) AND KARMA

#### DEFINITION

**Plain-language definition.** In the Indian traditions the moral and the religious are often not two departments. Right conduct *is* the order of things; and what you do is linked to what becomes of you, without anyone needing to keep a ledger.

**Technical definition.** **[FACT]** *Dharma* joins **normative order, social role, ritual duty and soteriological consequence**; karma connects conduct to future experience **without requiring a command in every school**. *Dharma* fuses the religious and the moral: right conduct *is* the cosmic-religious order, and karma makes morality soteriologically serious. **[FACT]** *Dharma*/*ṛta* treated as cosmic order means morality is not a constraint imposed on nature but the **grain** of it, so immorality is a form of disorder rather than merely a rule-violation.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> The Indian material is not decoration on a Western debate: it supplies the one thing the Western dependence question most lacks — a family of moral systems in which morality is neither commanded nor autonomously legislated, but is the structure of reality itself.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**dharma · normative order · role duty · ritual duty · soteriological consequence · ṛta as cosmic order · karma · morality as the grain of reality · *niṣkāma-karma* · *Gītā* 2.47 and 3.19 · devotion (*bhakti*) as a strand of the *Gītā* · exhaustive and impersonal accountability · role-*dharma* critique**

**How to use them:** the sentence that lifts an Indian paragraph out of the average band is "*dharma* is not simply morality — it can include ritual and role-specific duty, which is exactly why internal moral criticism is necessary". It shows you are using the concept rather than praising it.

#### VISUAL — WHAT *DHARMA* CONTAINS, AND WHAT KARMA DOES

```text
   ┌──────────────────────── DHARMA ────────────────────────┐
   │  four strands, NOT one moral theory                    │
   │  ┌────────────┬────────────┬────────────┬────────────┐ │
   │  │ NORMATIVE  │  SOCIAL    │  RITUAL    │ SOTERIO-   │ │
   │  │  ORDER     │   ROLE     │   DUTY     │ LOGICAL    │ │
   │  │            │            │            │ CONSEQUENCE│ │
   │  └────────────┴────────────┴────────────┴────────────┘ │
   │  ⇒ because ROLE and RITUAL are inside the concept,     │
   │    dharma is NOT identical with universal morality —   │
   │    which is why reformers criticise role-dharma using  │
   │    non-harm, equality, conscience and spiritual unity  │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

   RTA / DHARMA AS COSMIC ORDER          KARMA AS ACCOUNTABILITY
   morality = the GRAIN of reality       conduct → future experience
   immorality = DISORDER, not merely     · EXHAUSTIVE: no unobserved act
   a rule-violation                      · IMPERSONAL: no bribable judge
                                         · no COMMANDER required in
   THE GITA'S THREE STRANDS                some schools
   role-duty (svadharma)                          │
   + disciplined action (niskama-karma,           ▼
     2.47, 3.19: act, renounce the fruit)  ┌───────────────────────────┐
   + devotion (bhakti)                     │ CONTROL: karma does NOT   │
                                           │ authorise an observer to  │
                                           │ infer guilt from another's│
                                           │ suffering.                │
                                           └───────────────────────────┘
```

*The upper box is why *dharma* cannot be translated as "morality"; the lower left is why Indian ethics is not a constraint-ethics; the lower right is the moral safeguard that must accompany every use of karma in an answer.*

#### TEACHING

**[FACT] *Dharma* is four things at once.** Normative order, social role, ritual duty and soteriological consequence. ⚠️ The distinction that must be made: *dharma* is **not simply morality** — it can include ritual and role-specific duty, and that is precisely what makes internal moral criticism necessary rather than optional.

**[FACT] *Dharma* is not one moral theory.** Mīmāṃsā grounds ritual duty in injunction; the *Gītā* joins role-duty with disciplined action and devotion; Buddhism grounds conduct in intention, suffering and compassion; Jainism radicalises non-injury through vows; Nyāya and theistic Vedānta connect moral consequence to divine governance. Writing "Indian ethics says…" as though it were one voice is the error this list exists to prevent.

**[FACT] Karma and the fusion of morality with destiny.** Karma links moral action to spiritual destiny, which is why morality in these traditions is **soteriologically serious**: the stakes of conduct are not merely social. Karma can motivate accountability **without a divine commander** in some schools — which is exactly why it matters for the dependence question.

**[FACT] The safeguard.** ⚠️ Karma does **not** authorise observers to infer guilt from another's suffering. This control must accompany every use of karma in an answer; without it the doctrine is presented in a form that its own traditions criticise.

**[FACT] The *Gītā*'s distinctive move.** *Niṣkāma-karma* (*Gītā* 2.47, 3.19): one acts because the act is one's own duty (*svadharma*), having renounced the fruit — so the question "what do I get?" has already been removed from the deliberation. Devotion (*bhakti*) enters as one of the three strands by which the *Gītā* reworks duty, alongside role-duty and disciplined action; in this clause it is used as a **motivational and integrative** resource, not as an independent theory of moral grounding.

**[FACT] The standing objection and reply.** ⚠️ Role-*dharma* can preserve hierarchy. **Reformist reply:** appeal to deeper norms — non-harm, equality, conscience and spiritual unity — to criticise inherited practice. This is Session 9's internal-critique structure appearing inside the Indian material, and it should be labelled as such.

**[INFERENCE] Why cosmic order changes the shape of the debate.** If morality is the grain of reality rather than a rule imposed on it, then the Western fork — commanded or self-legislated — is not exhaustive. There is a third option: *discovered as the structure of what there is*. That option is not the same as natural law, because it does not require a divine reason grounding the order; and it is not the same as moral realism simpliciter, because the order is soteriologically loaded. Naming this third option is the highest-value contribution the Indian material makes to the clause.

**Objection.** Cosmic moral order is unverifiable. Karma cannot be observed, and an unobservable accountability mechanism is not an explanation but a promise.
**Reply.** **[INFERENCE]** The objection is correct as stated and should be conceded — unverifiability is the standing objection against this grounding, exactly as arbitrariness is against divine command and formalism against Kant. Two things can be said in mitigation. First, the parity point: the alternatives also rest on unverifiable commitments, such as the categorical bindingness of reason. Second, the *structural* claim survives the epistemic one: even a sceptic about karmic mechanics can recognise that a moral order conceived as exhaustive and impersonal answers the "all the time" problem in a way that a detection-dependent morality cannot.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH

> **Claim:** Indian *dharma* offers a third model of moral grounding in which morality is neither commanded nor self-legislated but is the order of reality itself.
> **Named evidence:** *Dharma* joins normative order, social role, ritual duty and soteriological consequence; karma links conduct to future experience without requiring a personal commander in several schools; and the *Gītā*'s *niṣkāma-karma* at 2.47 and 3.19 removes the fruit of action from deliberation altogether.
> **Analysis:** Because the order is conceived as exhaustive and impersonal, it dissolves rather than answers the problem of the undetected act, and because it requires no legislator it is untouched by the Euthyphro — so it is a genuinely distinct grounding rather than an Indian restatement of a Western one.
> **Qualification:** *Dharma* is not identical with universal morality, since role and ritual duty fall inside it and can preserve hierarchy; the reformist appeal to non-harm, equality, conscience and spiritual unity is the internal correction, and karma must never be used to infer guilt from another's suffering.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Do not identify *dharma* wholly with universal morality. Role and ritual duty are inside the concept, and the answer must say so before praising it.
2. ⚠️ Do not present Indian ethics as one system. Mīmāṃsā, the *Gītā*, Buddhism, Jainism and Nyāya ground conduct differently, and the differences are the examinable content.
3. ⚠️ Do not use karma to explain another person's misfortune. The doctrine motivates the agent's own accountability; it licenses no observer's inference about anyone else.

#### MINI RECAP

*Dharma* = order + role + ritual + soteriology, so not simply morality. *Ṛta*: morality as the grain of reality. Karma: exhaustive, impersonal, no commander needed — and no licence to judge another's suffering. *Gītā*: role-duty, *niṣkāma-karma*, devotion. Objection: unverifiability; reply: parity plus the structural point.

---

### SESSION 11 — INDIAN GROUNDS II: FOUR NON-THEISTIC GROUNDINGS OF ETHICS

#### DEFINITION

**Plain-language definition.** Can you build a real ethics with no God in it at all? Four traditions did — two of them Indian and ancient, long before the modern secular debate existed.

**Technical definition.** **[FACT]** The four non-theistic groundings that matter for parity are: **Buddhist** ethics grounded in intention (*cetanā*), the causal structure of suffering (*duḥkha*) and the cultivation of the four immeasurables (*brahmavihāra*: loving-kindness, compassion, sympathetic joy, equanimity); **Jain** ethics grounded in non-injury (*ahiṃsā*) as the supreme vow, graded through the lesser and greater vows (*aṇuvrata*/*mahāvrata*), with non-absolutism (*anekāntavāda*) functioning as an ethic of intellectual non-violence; **Mīmāṃsā** ethics grounded in injunction (*codanā*) alone, with no God and no consequentialist reduction; and **secular Western** ethics in its four families.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> The existence of four independently developed non-theistic groundings — two of them Indian and ancient — refutes the strong dependence thesis empirically as well as conceptually: this is not a possibility argued in a seminar but a fact about the history of ethics.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**intention (*cetanā*) · the four immeasurables (*brahmavihāra*) · Śāntideva · *Bodhicaryāvatāra* VIII · ownerless suffering · no substantial self (*anātman*) · conventional boundary between persons · non-injury (*ahiṃsā*) · lesser and greater vows (*aṇuvrata*/*mahāvrata*) · non-absolutism (*anekāntavāda*) as intellectual non-violence · injunction (*codanā*) · pure deontology without a personal commander**

**How to use them:** Śāntideva's argument is the single most valuable Indian citation in this clause. Give it as an *argument* in three steps, not as a name — an examiner can see immediately whether a candidate has the reasoning or only the reference.

#### VISUAL — FOUR GROUNDINGS, NO GOD REQUIRED

```text
  ┌───────────────┬──────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┐
  │ TRADITION     │ WHAT GROUNDS OBLIGATION      │ WHY IT NEEDS NO GOD     │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ BUDDHIST      │ intention (cetana), the      │ the ground is CAUSAL and│
  │               │ causal structure of suffering│ METAPHYSICAL, not       │
  │               │ + brahmaviharas              │ legislative             │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ JAIN          │ non-injury (ahimsa) as       │ obligation follows from │
  │               │ supreme vow; anuvrata /      │ the STATUS OF JIVAS,    │
  │               │ mahavrata grade it by        │ not from a command      │
  │               │ vocation; anekantavada =     │                         │
  │               │ intellectual non-violence    │                         │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ MIMAMSA       │ INJUNCTION (codana) alone —  │ authority WITHOUT a     │
  │               │ no God, no consequentialist  │ commander who is a      │
  │               │ reduction: a pure deontology │ person                  │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ SECULAR WEST  │ autonomy · welfare ·         │ no theistic premise in  │
  │               │ agreement · flourishing      │ any derivation          │
  └───────────────┴──────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┘

  SANTIDEVA'S ARGUMENT — Bodhicaryavatara VIII  (the strongest of the four)
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 1  There is no substantial self (anatman).                             │
  │ 2  Therefore suffering is OWNERLESS: it is not "mine" or "his" in any  │
  │    ultimate sense; the boundary between persons is a CONVENTIONAL      │
  │    construction.                                                       │
  │ 3  The reason to relieve MY future suffering is therefore exactly as   │
  │    strong as the reason to relieve ANOTHER'S.                          │
  │ ⇒  IMPARTIAL ALTRUISM grounded on a METAPHYSICAL premise, with no      │
  │    theological premise anywhere in the derivation.                     │
  │ STANDING OBJECTION: the argument depends on anatman being true.        │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

*The table is the parity evidence; the box is the argument you actually deploy. Three steps, one conclusion, one honest dependency.*

#### TEACHING

**[FACT] Buddhist grounding.** Ethics is grounded in **intention (*cetanā*)**, the causal structure of suffering (*duḥkha*), and the cultivation of the four immeasurables (*brahmavihāra*) — loving-kindness (*mettā*), compassion (*karuṇā*), sympathetic joy (*muditā*) and equanimity (*upekkhā*).

**[FACT] Śāntideva's argument.** In the *Bodhicaryāvatāra* (chapter VIII), the strongest philosophical piece in the set: since there is no substantial self, suffering is **ownerless**; the reason to relieve *my* future suffering is exactly as strong as the reason to relieve *another's*, since the boundary between them is a conventional construction. This grounds impartial altruism on a **metaphysical** rather than a theological premise, and is the single best Indian answer to "can ethics be grounded without God?".

**[FACT] Jain grounding.** Non-injury (*ahiṃsā*) as the supreme vow, extended to all living beings (*jīva*) in proportion to their sense-capacity; the lesser and greater vow structure (*aṇuvrata*/*mahāvrata*) grades obligation by vocation; and non-absolutism (*anekāntavāda*) functions as an **ethic of intellectual non-violence** — refusing to do epistemic harm by absolutising one's own standpoint.

**[FACT] Mīmāṃsā grounding.** Duty from **injunction alone (*codanā*)**, with no God and no consequentialist reduction — a pure deontology of the authoritative text, and a genuine counterexample to the claim that duty needs a commander who is a *person*. This is the sharpest of the four for the dependence question precisely because it retains scriptural authority while removing the personal legislator.

**[FACT] Secular Western grounding.** Kantian autonomy, utilitarian welfare, contractarian agreement and virtue-eudaimonism, each with its own well-known difficulty — formalism, demandingness, circularity, thick-nature dependence. Session 7 supplies the detail; here it is the fourth member of the parity set.

**[FACT] The comparative verdict.** ⚠️ The existence of four independently developed non-theistic groundings, two of them Indian and ancient, refutes the strong dependence thesis empirically as well as conceptually. What theistic ethics distinctively supplies is not the *content* of morality but a claimed unification of **obligation, motivation and cosmic significance** in a single source — and the price of that unification is the Euthyphro dilemma.

**[INFERENCE] How to rank the four in an answer.** Mīmāṃsā is the best counterexample to the *commander* requirement; Śāntideva is the best positive *argument* for impartial concern; the Jain vow-structure is the best model of *graded* obligation; the secular families are the best evidence of *plurality*. Choosing the right one for the stem, rather than listing all four, is what a 15-mark answer has room to do well.

**Objection.** These are not really non-theistic ethics. Mīmāṃsā rests on the authority of scripture, and Buddhism and Jainism rest on liberation-doctrines — so all three are religious ethics that happen to lack a creator, not evidence that ethics survives without religion.
**Reply.** **[INFERENCE]** Conceded in part, and the concession is important: none of the three is a *secular* ethics, and calling them secular is a real error. But the dependence thesis under examination is not "ethics requires no religious context"; it is that moral obligation is *grounded in God* or in divine command. Against that thesis, a scripturally authoritative but commander-free deontology is a decisive counterexample, and a liberation-ethics grounded in the causal structure of suffering is another. The reply concedes the classification and keeps the argument.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH

> **Claim:** Obligation can be grounded without God, and four independently developed traditions have in fact grounded it.
> **Named evidence:** Buddhist ethics grounds conduct in intention and the causal structure of suffering, with Śāntideva's *Bodhicaryāvatāra* VIII deriving impartial altruism from the ownerlessness of suffering; Jain ethics grounds obligation in non-injury and grades it through the lesser and greater vows; Mīmāṃsā grounds duty in injunction alone; and secular Western theories ground it in autonomy, welfare, agreement and flourishing.
> **Analysis:** Because Mīmāṃsā retains authority while removing the personal commander, and Śāntideva derives impartiality from a metaphysical rather than a theological premise, the strong dependence thesis is refuted empirically and not merely conceptually.
> **Qualification:** None of the Indian three is a secular ethics, and saying so is required; what they defeat is the claim that obligation must be grounded in God or in divine command, and Śāntideva's argument itself remains hostage to the truth of no-self.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Do not call the Indian non-theistic groundings "secular". They are religious ethics without a divine commander, and the distinction is examinable.
2. ⚠️ Do not cite Śāntideva as a name. Give the three-step argument — no substantial self, therefore ownerless suffering, therefore symmetrical reasons — or the citation earns nothing.
3. ⚠️ Do not present *anekāntavāda* as a theory of truth only. In this clause its examinable function is ethical: intellectual non-violence, the refusal to absolutise a standpoint.

#### MINI RECAP

Four groundings: intention and ownerless suffering (Buddhist), non-injury and graded vows (Jain), injunction without a commander (Mīmāṃsā), and the four secular families. What theism uniquely offers: obligation, motivation and cosmic significance unified in one source — priced at the Euthyphro.

---

### SESSION 12 — CAN MORALITY EXIST WITHOUT RELIGION? TESTING A NECESSITY CLAIM

#### DEFINITION

**Plain-language definition.** The question is not whether religious people are moral, nor whether morality historically grew up alongside religion. It is whether a *reference to God* has to appear somewhere in the account before an obligation can bind at all.

**Technical definition.** **[FACT]** The 2025 stem asks whether it is necessary for normative principles to bear reference to God in order to produce a feeling of obligation in a moral agent. Divine command theory says yes; Kant shows that **autonomy** can ground obligation without God. The verdict formula: **God-reference may strengthen motivation without constituting obligation.**

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> A necessity claim is refuted by a single working counterexample — and here there are five: Kantian autonomy, three secular families, and an ancient deontology that keeps scriptural authority while dispensing with a personal commander altogether.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**necessity claim · counterexample · grounding versus motivation versus epistemic access · feeling of obligation · autonomy · injunction without a commander · four independent non-theistic groundings · God-reference strengthens motivation without constituting obligation**

**How to use them:** notice the 2025 wording carefully — it says "in order to produce a **feeling of obligation** in a moral agent". That phrasing straddles grounding and motivation, and the highest-scoring move is to say so explicitly and then answer both halves.

#### VISUAL — HOW A NECESSITY CLAIM DIES

```text
   THE CLAIM: "normative principles MUST reference God to obligate"
                              │
              ┌───────────────┴────────────────┐
              ▼                                ▼
   READ AS GROUNDING                 READ AS MOTIVATION
   "reference to God is what         "reference to God is what makes
    MAKES the principle bind"         an agent FEEL bound"
              │                                │
              ▼                                ▼
   REFUTED BY COUNTEREXAMPLE:        NOT REFUTED — but not
   · Kant: self-legislated           NECESSARY either:
     universal law                   · duty, character, solidarity,
   · Mimamsa: injunction with          compassion, self-respect all
     NO personal commander             produce the felt bind
   · Santideva: ownerless            · and the religious route has a
     suffering                         COST: sanction-based motivation
   · secular families: welfare,        is heteronomous
     agreement, flourishing
              │                                │
              └───────────────┬────────────────┘
                              ▼
             VERDICT: God-reference may STRENGTHEN motivation
             without CONSTITUTING obligation.
             Necessity fails on both readings; sufficiency for
             motivation is granted, and priced.
```

*Split the stem before answering it. The left branch is killed by counterexample; the right branch is granted as influence and then priced. That two-branch structure is the answer.*

#### TEACHING

**[FACT] What kind of claim is being tested.** "Must normative principles reference God to create obligation?" is a **necessity** claim. Divine command theory says yes; Kant shows that autonomy can ground obligation without God.

**[FACT] The counterexamples, in order of strength.** (1) **Kant**: obligation from rational self-legislation, with God, freedom and immortality as postulates rather than as the source of the law. (2) **Mīmāṃsā**: duty from injunction alone, retaining authority while removing the personal commander — the sharpest counterexample because it concedes most to the theist and still wins. (3) **Śāntideva**: impartial altruism from the ownerlessness of suffering. (4) **The secular families**: welfare, agreement, flourishing, dignity.

**[FACT] The verdict formula.** ⚠️ "Morality is not **conceptually** dependent on religion — the Euthyphro shows that, and four independent non-theistic groundings confirm it. It may be **motivationally** and **historically** dependent for many agents, and those are different claims that a good answer keeps apart."

**[INFERENCE] The "feeling of obligation" wrinkle.** The 2025 wording is unusually precise and rewards a candidate who notices it. A *feeling* of obligation is a psychological state, so on its face the stem is about motivation. But "normative principles bear reference to God **in order to produce**" implies a claimed necessary condition, which is a grounding-shaped claim about a psychological effect. The correct handling is to split it: as a claim about what makes principles binding it is false; as a claim about what makes agents feel bound it is false as a *necessity* claim and true only as a claim about *one available and effective route*, whose cost is heteronomy.

**[FACT] What the theist may still say.** ⚠️ God-reference may strengthen motivation without constituting obligation. That is a real concession to the religious side, and making it is what converts a one-sided script into a graded one.

**Objection.** Even Kant's morality is parasitic: without the postulates of God, freedom and immortality, the moral life is incoherent on his own account — so reference to God has not been eliminated after all.
**Reply.** **[INFERENCE]** The premise is accurate and worth conceding, but it does not carry the conclusion. The postulates answer a question morality *raises* — whether virtue and happiness are permanently divorced — and not the question of what makes the moral law binding. Kant's law binds in virtue of its form, and an agent who suspends judgement on the postulates is not thereby released from the categorical imperative. Reference to God completes a moral *outlook* on this account; it does not constitute an obligation.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH

> **Claim:** Normative principles need not refer to God in order to obligate, because obligation has been grounded without any theistic premise in several independent traditions.
> **Named evidence:** Kant grounds obligation in rational self-legislation and reduces God to a practical postulate; Mīmāṃsā grounds duty in injunction alone, with no commander who is a person; Śāntideva grounds impartial altruism in the ownerlessness of suffering; and the secular families ground it in welfare, agreement and flourishing.
> **Analysis:** Since a necessity claim falls to a single working counterexample, four independent ones defeat it decisively, and the Mīmāṃsā case is the most damaging because it retains scriptural authority — the theist's own preferred kind of ground — while removing the personal legislator.
> **Qualification:** What survives is the weaker and true claim that reference to God can *strengthen* the felt bind of obligation for many agents; that concession must be made, and it must be priced, since motivation supplied by sanction is heteronomous and forfeits moral worth on Kant's account.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Do not answer a necessity stem with a survey of positions. Identify the claim as a necessity claim and produce counterexamples; that structure *is* the answer.
2. ⚠️ Do not ignore the exact wording "feeling of obligation". It straddles grounding and motivation, and splitting it is where the top marks are.
3. ⚠️ Do not use the sentence commonly paraphrased as "without God everything is permitted" as a verified quotation from Dostoevsky. It is a summary of a theme, not a verbatim line.

#### MINI RECAP

Necessity claim. Split into grounding and motivation. Grounding: refuted by Kant, Mīmāṃsā, Śāntideva and the secular families. Motivation: not necessary, sometimes effective, and priced at heteronomy. Formula: strengthens motivation without constituting obligation.

---

### SESSION 13 — CAN THERE BE RELIGION WITHOUT MORALITY? THE ASYMMETRY DEFENDED

#### DEFINITION

**Plain-language definition.** The slogan runs: you can be moral without being religious, but you cannot have a religion with no morality in it. The interesting part is *why* that asymmetry holds — and it is not the reason most candidates give.

**Technical definition.** **[FACT]** *Normatively adequate* religion without morality is difficult to defend, because religion makes claims about worthy life, duty, transformation or liberation. Yet a *descriptively identifiable* religious institution can transmit immoral commands or practices; calling it immoral does not make it sociologically nonexistent. Meanwhile **morality without religion is possible**, since secular ethics grounds morality in reason or consequences rather than in God. That is the asymmetry.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> The asymmetry holds, but for a reason the slogan hides: morality is *constitutive* of what we are willing to call a religion, whereas religion is at most *supportive* of morality — and constitutive and supportive are not two degrees of one relation.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**asymmetry · constitutive versus supportive · normatively adequate religion · descriptively identifiable institution · every religion prescribes an ethic · internal link · secular ethics · the two directions tested separately**

**How to use them:** the phrase "descriptively identifiable versus normatively adequate" is the single distinction that rescues this answer from incoherence. Without it a candidate is forced either to deny that immoral religious institutions exist or to abandon the asymmetry.

#### VISUAL — TWO DIRECTIONS, TESTED SEPARATELY

```text
   DIRECTION 1: MORALITY WITHOUT RELIGION?          → POSSIBLE
   ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │ Kant's autonomy · utilitarian welfare · contractarian          │
   │ agreement · humanist dignity · Mimamsa injunction ·            │
   │ Santideva's ownerless suffering · Jain vows                    │
   │ ⇒ working systems, several of them ancient                     │
   └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

   DIRECTION 2: RELIGION WITHOUT MORALITY?          → SPLIT THE QUESTION
   ┌──────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────┐
   │ NORMATIVELY ADEQUATE religion │ DESCRIPTIVELY IDENTIFIABLE     │
   │ ── very hard to defend        │ institution                    │
   │ every religion makes claims   │ ── plainly CAN transmit        │
   │ about worthy life, duty,      │ immoral commands or practices  │
   │ transformation or liberation  │ calling it immoral does not    │
   │ ⇒ an ethic is INTERNAL to     │ make it sociologically         │
   │   the concept                 │ nonexistent                    │
   └──────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────┘

   WHY THE ASYMMETRY IS NOT A MATTER OF DEGREE
        MORALITY → RELIGION : CONSTITUTIVE  (remove it and the thing
                              stops qualifying as what it claims to be)
        RELIGION → MORALITY : SUPPORTIVE    (remove it and morality
                              loses a resource, not its foundation)
```

*Test the two directions in separate paragraphs; split direction 2 into the normative and descriptive readings; then state the constitutive/supportive contrast as the explanation. That is the whole architecture of a 2022- or 2024-type answer.*

#### TEACHING

**[FACT] Direction one — morality without religion.** Possible. Secular ethics (Kant, utilitarianism, humanism) grounds morality in reason or consequences rather than in God, and the Indian non-theistic groundings of Session 11 extend the point historically.

**[FACT] Direction two — religion without morality.** Split it. *Normatively adequate* religion without morality is difficult to defend, because religion makes claims about worthy life, duty, transformation or liberation — an ethic is internal to the concept. But a *descriptively identifiable* religious institution can transmit immoral commands or practices, and calling it immoral does not make it sociologically nonexistent.

**[FACT] The distinction that must be made in the 2024 stem.** ⚠️ In "religion without morality", distinguish descriptive institutions from normatively adequate religion. Without that distinction the candidate must either deny visible facts or abandon the asymmetry, and both are marked down.

**[INFERENCE] Why the asymmetry is not a difference of degree.** Morality is *constitutive* of what we are willing to call a religion: remove the claim about how one ought to live and the remainder no longer answers to the concept. Religion is at most *supportive* of morality: remove it and morality loses a motivational and formative resource, not its foundation. A constitutive relation and a supportive relation are different in kind, and saying so is what makes the answer explanatory rather than descriptive.

**[FACT] The full assessment for a 2022-type stem.** The Euthyphro blocks strong divine command theory; religion nevertheless motivates and sustains morality; therefore the asymmetry holds — morality is autonomous, and religion is ethically constituted.

**[INFERENCE] The hardest counterexample, and how to take it.** Consider a tradition whose moral content is minimal or whose ritual observance is treated as sufficient without ethical demand. Is that a religion without morality? The honest answer is that it is a religion with a *thin and contested* morality, not with none: even ritual-centred systems specify who may perform what, when, and with what disposition, and those are normative claims about a worthy life. What the case genuinely shows is that the ethical content of a religion can be minimal, contested and internally criticised — which is different from absent, and which is exactly what the prophetic critique of Session 9 documents.

**Objection.** If an immoral institution still counts as a religion descriptively, the asymmetry is merely verbal: it says only that we *choose* not to call a moral-free system a proper religion.
**Reply.** **[INFERENCE]** The objection would succeed if the concept were arbitrary, but it is not. The claim is that a system asserting nothing about how one ought to live could not perform the functions by which religion is identified in the first place — it could not orient a life, prescribe a path, or specify transformation or liberation. The constraint is functional, not stipulative, and that is what makes the asymmetry a finding rather than a preference.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH

> **Claim:** The asymmetry in the slogan holds, but only once "religion without morality" is split into its normative and descriptive readings.
> **Named evidence:** Morality without religion is exhibited by Kantian autonomy, utilitarian welfare and humanist dignity, and by the ancient non-theistic groundings of Mīmāṃsā, Buddhism and Jainism; religion without morality is defensible descriptively, since institutions can transmit immoral practice, but not normatively, since every religion makes claims about worthy life, duty, transformation or liberation.
> **Analysis:** The explanation of the asymmetry is that morality is *constitutive* of what qualifies as a religion while religion is only *supportive* of morality, and a constitutive relation cannot be reduced to a stronger degree of a supportive one.
> **Qualification:** The constraint is functional rather than stipulative — a system asserting nothing about how one ought to live could not orient a life at all — and it leaves fully open that a religion's ethical content may be thin, contested and subject to internal criticism.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ In "religion without morality", always distinguish descriptive institutions from normatively adequate religion. Failing to split it is the standard way this question is lost.
2. ⚠️ Do not defend the asymmetry by asserting that religious people behave better. The asymmetry is conceptual, and a behavioural defence of it invites the correlation trap.
3. ⚠️ Do not treat "constitutive" and "supportive" as strong and weak versions of one relation. The whole explanatory value of the distinction is that they are different in kind.

#### MINI RECAP

Direction 1: possible, and demonstrated. Direction 2: split normative from descriptive. Explanation: constitutive versus supportive. Verdict: the asymmetry holds — morality is autonomous, religion is ethically constituted.

---

### SESSION 14 — "WHY BE MORAL — ALL THE TIME?": THE PROBLEM IN ITS CLASSICAL FORMS

#### DEFINITION

**Plain-language definition.** Everyone can explain why we should be moral in general. The hard case is the one where nobody will ever know, it costs you something real, and no one will repay you. That is where "all the time" bites.

**Technical definition.** **[FACT]** The printed stem is "Secular ethics cannot fully resolve as to **why one should be moral all the time**." The load is carried by "all the time": the hard case is morality when it is **costly, undetected and unenforced**. The classical statements are Glaucon's **Ring of Gyges**, Hobbes's **Foole**, Hume's **sensible knave**, and the meta-level challenges of **Bradley** and **Prichard** that the question may be malformed.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> The weight of this question is carried by three words — "all the time" — because every ethical theory can explain the ordinary case, and only the undetected, costly, unreciprocated act separates the theories that answer the question from the theories that restate it.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**"all the time" · costly, undetected, unenforced · Ring of Gyges (*Republic* II 359d–360b) · Hobbes's Foole · Hume's sensible knave (*Enquiry* IX.ii) · inward peace of mind · Bradley, *Ethical Studies* Essay II · Prichard, "Does Moral Philosophy Rest on a Mistake?" (1912) · self-defeating question · justification versus motivation**

**How to use them:** quote the operative phrase from the stem in your first line and build the whole answer on it. Examiners reward fidelity to the exact scope of a claim more reliably than they reward extra content.

#### VISUAL — THE FOUR CLASSICAL STATEMENTS, AND THE META-CHALLENGE

```text
   THE HARD CASE:  costly  +  undetected  +  unreciprocated
                              │
   ┌──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │ GLAUCON'S RING OF GYGES      Republic II 359d-360b                  │
   │ Given invisibility, would anyone remain just?                       │
   │ ⇒ the challenge is to show justice is good IN ITSELF, not for its   │
   │   reputation.                                                       │
   ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
   │ HOBBES'S FOOLE                                                      │
   │ Says in his heart there is no justice; covenant-breaking is         │
   │ rational when it pays.                                              │
   ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
   │ HUME'S SENSIBLE KNAVE        Enquiry IX.ii                          │
   │ Observes the rules generally, makes exceptions when they profit     │
   │ and go undetected. Hume ADMITS he finds the knave hard to answer,   │
   │ and falls back on the "inward peace of mind" and integrity the      │
   │ knave forfeits.                                                     │
   └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                              │
                              ▼
   THE META-CHALLENGE: IS THE QUESTION EVEN WELL-FORMED?
   ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │ BRADLEY, Ethical Studies, Essay II, "Why Should I Be Moral?"        │
   │ The question may be SELF-DEFEATING: to ask for a NON-MORAL reason   │
   │ to be moral is to ask morality to justify itself before a tribunal  │
   │ it does not recognise.                                              │
   │ PRICHARD, "Does Moral Philosophy Rest on a Mistake?" (1912)         │
   │ Demanding a SELF-INTERESTED justification of obligation             │
   │ MISCONSTRUES obligation.                                            │
   └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

*The three boxes at the top are the problem; the box at the bottom is the move that dissolves rather than answers it. A strong script states both, and then says which it is using.*

#### TEACHING

**[FACT] Glaucon's Ring of Gyges.** *Republic* II 359d–360b: given invisibility, would anyone remain just? The challenge is to show that justice is good *in itself* and not merely for the reputation it earns. It is the canonical statement of the "all the time" problem, and it is a *challenge* rather than a thesis: Glaucon does not believe it, he demands an answer to it.

**[FACT] Hobbes's Foole.** The one who says in his heart that there is no justice, and that covenant-breaking is rational when it pays. The Foole converts Glaucon's thought-experiment into a standing question for any contract-based ethics.

**[FACT] Hume's sensible knave.** *Enquiry Concerning the Principles of Morals* IX.ii: the person who observes the rules generally but makes exceptions when they profit and go undetected. ⚠️ The examinable detail is that **Hume admits he finds the knave hard to answer**, and falls back on the "inward peace of mind" and integrity that the knave forfeits. A candidate who reports Hume's own difficulty honestly is showing exactly the quality the question is testing.

**[FACT] Bradley's meta-challenge.** *Ethical Studies*, Essay II, "Why Should I Be Moral?": the question may be **self-defeating**, because to ask for a *non-moral* reason to be moral is to ask morality to justify itself before a tribunal it does not recognise.

**[FACT] Prichard's parallel point.** "Does Moral Philosophy Rest on a Mistake?" (*Mind*, 1912): demanding a self-interested justification of obligation misconstrues what obligation is.

**[INFERENCE] How to use the meta-challenge without evading the stem.** Bradley and Prichard are not a way of refusing to answer. Deploy them as a *result*: the question, taken as a demand for a prudential reason, is malformed; taken as a question about **motivation in the hard case**, it is perfectly well formed and genuinely difficult. That reframing is what allows the answer to concede the printed claim's real force while denying its stated scope.

**[INFERENCE] Why this session is separated from its answers.** The problem has to be stated at full strength before any theory is offered, because the commonest failure here is to answer a weakened version — "why be moral?" instead of "why be moral *all the time*?" — and then declare the difficulty solved. Session 15 supplies the answers; this session's whole task is to make sure they are answering the right question.

**Objection.** The Ring of Gyges is a fantasy, the Foole is a caricature, and the perfect knave never exists: in real life dispositions leak, reputations form, and concealment fails. So the problem is artificial.
**Reply.** **[INFERENCE]** The empirical point is largely true and should be granted — which is precisely why the contractarian reply in Session 15 has real force. But the artificiality does not dissolve the philosophical question. The thought-experiment isolates the *structure* of the justification: it asks whether morality's claim on an agent depends on detectability. If it does, morality is a policy rather than an obligation; and showing that detection is usually likely does not show that the claim was never conditional on it.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH

> **Claim:** The printed claim's force lies entirely in the phrase "all the time", which isolates the costly, undetected and unreciprocated act.
> **Named evidence:** Glaucon's Ring of Gyges at *Republic* II 359d–360b asks whether justice survives invisibility; Hobbes's Foole holds covenant-breaking rational when it pays; Hume's sensible knave makes undetected exceptions, and Hume himself concedes the difficulty, retreating to the inward peace of mind the knave forfeits.
> **Analysis:** Because each case removes detection, reciprocity and cost-freeness together, they test whether a theory grounds obligation unconditionally or merely recommends a reliable policy, which is a sharper test than the general question "why be moral?".
> **Qualification:** Bradley and Prichard press the further point that a demand for a non-moral reason to be moral may be malformed; the right use of that move is to accept it against the prudential reading while conceding that the motivational question in the hard case remains real and unanswered.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Do not answer "why be moral?" when the stem says "all the time". The operative phrase is the question, and losing it is the standard way this stem is misread.
2. ⚠️ Do not present Hume as having answered the knave. He records his own difficulty, and reporting that accurately is worth more than a tidy resolution he did not offer.
3. ⚠️ Do not use Bradley and Prichard to dodge the stem. Use them to reframe it: malformed as a prudential demand, well formed as a motivational one.

#### MINI RECAP

Three words carry the question. Gyges: invisibility. The Foole: profitable breach. The knave: undetected exceptions, and Hume's own admission. Bradley and Prichard: the demand may be malformed. Answers come next — the point of this session is that they must answer *this* question.

---

### SESSION 15 — THE ANSWERS TO "ALL THE TIME", AND WHAT EACH ONE ACTUALLY BUYS

#### DEFINITION

**Plain-language definition.** Two families of answer are on offer. The secular family says the question is confused, or that being moral pays, or that it is part of a good life. The religious family says the undetected case does not exist.

**Technical definition.** **[FACT]** The secular answers are Kantian (the categorical imperative is not conditional on desire), contractarian (Gauthier's **constrained maximisation**), eudaimonist (morality is partly constitutive of flourishing) and sentimentalist-evolutionary (sympathy, reciprocity and reputation). The religious answers are **divine sanction**, **divine love**, **karma**, ***niṣkāma-karma*** and **cosmic order (*dharma*/*ṛta*)**. The assessment turns on a single distinction: **justification versus motivation**.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> The claim is half true, and the half that is true is motivational, not logical: secular ethics resolves the justificatory question as well as any theory can, and it is weaker only on motivation in the undetected, costly case — where religion's advantage is scope, purchased at the price of heteronomy if it works through sanction.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**justification versus motivation · categorical, not conditional · constrained maximisation (Gauthier) · detectability of dispositions · eudaimonism · thick account of human nature · sympathy and reciprocity · exception clause for the undetected case · divine sanction · heteronomy · divine love · karma's exhaustiveness · *niṣkāma-karma* · morality as the grain of reality**

**How to use them:** the mark-winning sentence is "secular ethics answers the justificatory question; the printed doubt bites only on motivation in the undetected case". Write it, then defend both halves. It concedes exactly enough to look fair and denies exactly enough to be a verdict.

#### VISUAL — FOUR SECULAR ANSWERS, FIVE RELIGIOUS ANSWERS, AND WHERE EACH STOPS

```text
  SECULAR ANSWERS                          WHERE EACH ONE STOPS
  ┌──────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ KANTIAN                      │ explains why the moral law BINDS, not  │
  │ the question is confused: a  │ why THIS agent will act on it when     │
  │ categorical imperative is by │ inclination pulls hard                 │
  │ definition not conditional   │                                        │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ CONTRACTARIAN (Hobbes,       │ works where DISPOSITIONS ARE           │
  │ Gauthier): constrained       │ DETECTABLE; the perfect knave with a   │
  │ maximisation — being disposed│ perfect mask remains unanswered        │
  │ to keep agreements is itself │                                        │
  │ the utility-maximising       │                                        │
  │ DISPOSITION                  │                                        │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ EUDAIMONIST (Aristotle)      │ presupposes a THICK account of human   │
  │ being moral is partly        │ nature which the sceptic can decline   │
  │ CONSTITUTIVE of flourishing, │                                        │
  │ so the question mistakes a   │                                        │
  │ component of the good life   │                                        │
  │ for a cost                   │                                        │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ SENTIMENTALIST/EVOLUTIONARY  │ explaining a MOTIVE is not justifying  │
  │ sympathy, reciprocity,       │ a NORM — and reciprocity has an        │
  │ reputation                   │ EXPLICIT EXCEPTION CLAUSE for the      │
  │                              │ undetected case                        │
  └──────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────┘

  RELIGIOUS ANSWERS                        WHAT EACH ONE ADDS / COSTS
  ┌──────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 1 DIVINE SANCTION            │ the undetected case does not exist —   │
  │                              │ COST: prudential motivation is         │
  │                              │ HETERONOMOUS and, on Kant's lights,    │
  │                              │ morally worthless                      │
  │ 2 DIVINE LOVE / RELATIONSHIP │ motivates by love of the good, not     │
  │                              │ fear — ESCAPES the heteronomy objection│
  │ 3 KARMA                      │ EXHAUSTIVE and IMPERSONAL: no          │
  │                              │ unobserved act, no bribable judge, no  │
  │                              │ escape by concealment, no gap between  │
  │                              │ moral and causal order                 │
  │                              │ ⇒ Gyges' ring has NO PURCHASE          │
  │ 4 NISKAMA-KARMA (Gita 2.47,  │ DISSOLVES the question: the fruit is   │
  │   3.19)                      │ removed from deliberation, so          │
  │                              │ "what do I get?" is not asked          │
  │ 5 DHARMA / RTA as cosmic     │ morality is the GRAIN of reality, not  │
  │   order                      │ a constraint on it; immorality is      │
  │                              │ DISORDER                               │
  └──────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────┘

  THE ASSESSMENT
  JUSTIFICATION ──── secular ethics answers this as well as any theory can
  MOTIVATION ─────── the printed claim bites HERE, and only here
  religion's advantage = SCOPE (an order coextensive with reality)
  religion's cost     = if it works through SANCTION, it wins the motivation
                        and loses the moral worth
```

*Read the two tables as claim and bill. The assessment strip at the foot is the answer to the printed stem, and it is the sentence to write last.*

#### TEACHING

**[FACT] The Kantian answer, and its limit.** The question is confused: a categorical imperative is by definition not conditional on desire, and a rational agent legislating universally is not asking "what is in it for me?". *Limit:* it explains why the moral law binds, not why *this* agent will act on it when inclination pulls hard.

**[FACT] The contractarian answer, and its limit.** Morality is the rational output of mutually advantageous constraint; Gauthier's **constrained maximisation** argues that being *disposed* to keep agreements is itself the utility-maximising disposition. *Limit:* the disposition-argument works where dispositions are detectable; the perfect knave with a perfect mask remains unanswered.

**[FACT] The eudaimonist answer, and its limit.** Being moral is partly **constitutive** of flourishing, so "why be moral?" mistakes a component of the good life for a cost. *Limit:* it presupposes a thick account of human nature that the sceptic can decline.

**[FACT] The sentimentalist and evolutionary answer, and its limit.** Sympathy, reciprocity and reputation explain both the origin and the motive. *Limit:* explaining a motive is not justifying a norm — and reciprocity has an **explicit exception clause** for the undetected case, which is the very case at issue.

**[FACT] Religious answer one — divine sanction.** The undetected case does not exist, because God sees. *Limit:* prudential motivation is **heteronomous** and, by Kant's lights, morally worthless — a serious cost, not a quibble.

**[FACT] Religious answer two — divine love and relationship.** Mature theism motivates by love of the good and of God rather than by fear; this **escapes** the heteronomy objection, and it is the version a strong answer should attribute to the theist rather than the sanction version.

**[FACT] Religious answer three — karma.** The strongest Indian answer to the "all the time" clause. Karma is **exhaustive and impersonal**: there is no unobserved act, no bribable judge, no escape by concealment, and no gap between the moral order and the causal order. The Ring of Gyges has no purchase where invisibility to *persons* is irrelevant.

**[FACT] Religious answer four — *niṣkāma-karma*.** *Gītā* 2.47 and 3.19: the deepest Indian move is to **dissolve** the question — one acts because the act is one's own duty (*svadharma*), having renounced the fruit, so "what do I get?" has already been removed from the deliberation.

**[FACT] Religious answer five — *dharma* and cosmic order (*ṛta*).** ⚠️ Morality is not a constraint imposed on nature but the *grain* of it, so immorality is a form of disorder rather than merely a rule-violation.

**[FACT] The assessment.** ⚠️ The distinction that must be drawn is between **justification** and **motivation**. Secular ethics can *justify* moral norms — Kant, contractarianism and virtue ethics all supply grounds. Where the printed claim has force is on **motivation in the hard case**: the undetected, costly, unreciprocated act. Religion's distinctive contribution there is **scope** — a moral order coextensive with reality, from which nothing is exempt — and that is a motivational, not a logical, advantage. ⚠️ The counter-cost is equally real: a motivation grounded in sanction is morally inferior to one grounded in the good itself, so the religious advantage is purchased at the price the Euthyphro and heteronomy objections identify.

**[INFERENCE] Why karma is the strongest of the five.** Answers one and two depend on a personal God, and answer one incurs the heteronomy charge. Karma incurs neither: it is impersonal, so there is no judge to bribe or to obey heteronomously, and it is exhaustive, so the hard case is not a special case. Its bill is unverifiability, which is a different and arguably lighter cost than moral worthlessness. In a fifteen-mark answer on this stem, karma deserves more space than divine sanction.

**Objection.** Karma answers the question only by making morality prudential on a cosmic scale: act well because the universe keeps accounts. That is Gyges with a bigger ring, not an escape from him.
**Reply.** **[INFERENCE]** A serious objection, and the reply is *niṣkāma-karma*. If the doctrine is presented as cosmic bookkeeping, the objection lands squarely. But the *Gītā*'s development at 2.47 and 3.19 explicitly removes the fruit of action from deliberation, so the agent is not acting *in order to* secure a karmic return. The prudential reading is available and is criticised from inside the tradition; the developed doctrine is not prudential at all, and citing 2.47 is what shows the difference.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH

> **Claim:** The printed claim is half true: secular ethics resolves the justificatory question and is weaker only on motivation in the undetected, costly case.
> **Named evidence:** Kant makes obligation unconditional but not self-executing; Gauthier's constrained maximisation works only where dispositions are detectable; eudaimonism requires a thick human nature the sceptic may decline; reciprocity carries an explicit exception for the undetected act — while karma is exhaustive and impersonal and *niṣkāma-karma* at *Gītā* 2.47 removes the fruit from deliberation altogether.
> **Analysis:** Since every secular answer stops precisely at the case the stem isolates, the claim has real force; but the force is motivational rather than logical, because none of those stopping-points is a failure of justification.
> **Qualification:** Religion's advantage is scope rather than validity, and it is priced: motivation grounded in sanction is heteronomous and forfeits moral worth, so only the divine-love and *niṣkāma-karma* versions win the motivational argument without incurring the moral cost.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ In "why be moral", separate **justification** from **motivation**. The printed claim is weak on the first and strong on the second, and saying so *is* the answer.
2. ⚠️ Do not give the theist only the sanction answer. Divine love and *niṣkāma-karma* are stronger versions and attributing only the weakest one is a straw man.
3. ⚠️ Do not present karma as cosmic reward. Cite 2.47 and 3.19 to show that the developed doctrine removes the fruit from deliberation, or the prudential objection stands unanswered.

#### MINI RECAP

Four secular answers, four stopping points. Five religious answers: sanction (heteronomous), love (escapes it), karma (exhaustive, impersonal), *niṣkāma-karma* (dissolves the question), cosmic order (morality as the grain of reality). Verdict: half true — and the true half is motivational.

---

### SESSION 16 — RELIGIOSITY AND "IMMORALITY": THE PRINTED 2018 STEM

#### DEFINITION

**Plain-language definition.** The question is not "is religion moral?" It is whether *being religious* — the disposition, the observance, the intensity — is systematically bound up with moral failure.

**Technical definition.** **[FACT]** ❓ The held 2018 paper visibly prints "**immorality**"; the module treats that printed term and does not silently regularise it to "morality". ⚠️ The stem asks whether **religiosity** — a psychological and behavioural disposition — is systematically connected with **moral failure**. It is not the same as "is religion moral?": the subject is *religiosity*, the disposition, not *religion*, the system.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> There is a genuine inter-relatedness, but it is **conditional, not necessary**: religiosity does not produce immorality, it magnifies the moral content it is attached to — and the traditions' own prophetic critiques show that they know it.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**religiosity as a disposition, not a system · the printed word retained · ritual-substitution argument · prophetic critique · in-group / out-group · Gordon Allport · intrinsic religiosity · extrinsic religiosity · Daniel Batson · quest orientation · teleological suspension of the ethical · scrupulosity · moral licensing · amplifier, not aligner · conditional ambivalence**

**How to use them:** the intrinsic / extrinsic / quest apparatus is the analytical spine of this answer. Naming Allport and Batson converts a discussion into an analysis, and it explains in one move why "religiosity" as such correlates with nothing simple.

#### VISUAL — FOUR ARGUMENTS FOR, THREE AGAINST, ONE VERDICT

```text
  ARGUMENTS THAT RELIGIOSITY CAN GENERATE IMMORALITY
  ┌───┬────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 1 │ RITUAL SUBSTITUTION│ observance displaces moral substance.       │
  │   │                    │ THE EVIDENCE IS INTERNAL: Hebrew prophets   │
  │   │                    │ denounce festivals and sacrifice offered    │
  │   │                    │ without justice (Amos 5, Isaiah 1); the     │
  │   │                    │ Buddha criticises Vedic sacrifice as        │
  │   │                    │ ineffective; Kabir and Nanak attack         │
  │   │                    │ ritualism and caste-observance; the Gita    │
  │   │                    │ (2.42-43) disparages those enchanted by     │
  │   │                    │ "flowery words" of ritual reward.           │
  │   │                    │ ⇒ that EVERY tradition contains this        │
  │   │                    │   critique is itself the evidence that the  │
  │   │                    │   danger is real.                           │
  ├───┼────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 2 │ IN-GROUP /         │ religiosity binds by MARKING A BOUNDARY,    │
  │   │ OUT-GROUP          │ and boundary-marking has a predictable      │
  │   │                    │ moral cost toward outsiders.                │
  │   │                    │ APPARATUS: Allport's INTRINSIC (religion as │
  │   │                    │ an end in itself) vs EXTRINSIC (religion as │
  │   │                    │ an instrument for status, security or       │
  │   │                    │ belonging); Batson adds the QUEST           │
  │   │                    │ orientation. Moral correlations differ      │
  │   │                    │ SHARPLY by orientation — which is why       │
  │   │                    │ "religiosity" as such correlates with       │
  │   │                    │ nothing simple.                             │
  ├───┼────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 3 │ SUSPENSION         │ if religious duty can OVERRIDE ordinary     │
  │   │                    │ ethics, religiosity supplies a route to     │
  │   │                    │ acts that are, judged ethically, immoral —  │
  │   │                    │ Kierkegaard's teleological suspension of    │
  │   │                    │ the ethical is the honest statement of it.  │
  ├───┼────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 4 │ SCRUPULOSITY /     │ pathological guilt on one side; MORAL       │
  │   │ LICENSING          │ LICENSING ("I have observed, therefore I am │
  │   │                    │ good") on the other.                        │
  └───┴────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ARGUMENTS AGAINST A SYSTEMATIC CONNECTION
  1 every tradition also generates reformers, ascetics and moral exemplars,
    so the correlation is with ORIENTATION, not with religiosity
  2 the immoral religionist is judged by the tradition's OWN norms —
    INTERNAL CORRUPTION, not a counter-example to religious ethics
  3 COMPARISON CLASS: secular ideologies have generated comparable moral
    failures, so religiosity is not the differentiating variable

  THE VERDICT: religiosity is MORALLY AMBIVALENT, not morally aligned.
  It AMPLIFIES (i) whatever moral content the religion supplies and
  (ii) whatever motive the individual brings.
  Its ethical value is therefore a function of:
     (i)  whether the religion's ULTIMATE IS ITSELF MORALISED
          (a God whose nature is goodness; a dharma that is justice)
     (ii) whether the individual's orientation is INTRINSIC or EXTRINSIC
```

*Four arguments up, three arguments back, one conditional verdict. The verdict is the only sentence in this session an examiner will look for twice.*

#### TEACHING

**[FACT] Reading the stem.** ⚠️ "Is there an inter-relatedness between religiosity and immorality?" asks whether *being religious* is systematically connected with moral failure. The subject is the disposition, not the system, and getting that right in the first sentence is half the answer.

**[FACT] The ritual-substitution argument.** Religiosity can displace moral substance with observance. This is the standing **prophetic critique inside the traditions**: the Hebrew prophets denounce festivals and sacrifices offered without justice (Amos 5, Isaiah 1); the Buddha criticises Vedic sacrifice as ineffective; Kabīr and Nānak attack ritualism and caste-observance; the *Gītā* (2.42–43) disparages those enchanted by the "flowery words" of ritual reward. ✅ That every tradition contains this critique is itself the evidence that the danger is real.

**[FACT] The in-group / out-group argument, and the standard apparatus.** Religiosity binds a community by marking a boundary, and boundary-marking has a predictable moral cost toward outsiders. **Gordon Allport's** distinction between **intrinsic** religiosity (religion as an end in itself) and **extrinsic** religiosity (religion as an instrument for status, security or belonging) — with **Daniel Batson's** later addition of the **quest** orientation — is the standard analytical apparatus: the moral correlations differ sharply by orientation, which is why "religiosity" as such correlates with nothing simple.

**[FACT] The suspension argument.** If religious duty can override ordinary ethics, religiosity supplies a route to acts that are, judged ethically, immoral. Kierkegaard's **teleological suspension of the ethical** is the honest philosophical statement of this, and it is honest precisely because Kierkegaard concedes that the agent cannot make himself intelligible.

**[FACT] The scrupulosity and licensing arguments.** Religiosity can produce pathological guilt on one side and **moral licensing** — "I have observed, therefore I am good" — on the other. The two failures are opposite in direction and both are real.

**[FACT] The three arguments against a systematic connection.** (1) Every tradition also generates reformers, ascetics and moral exemplars, so the correlation is not with religiosity but with **its orientation**. (2) The immoral religionist is judged by the tradition's own norms — the phenomenon is **internal corruption**, not a counter-example to religious ethics. (3) The comparison class matters: secular ideologies have generated comparable moral failures, so "religiosity" is not the differentiating variable.

**[FACT] The precise verdict.** ⚠️ Religiosity is **morally ambivalent, not morally aligned**: it *amplifies* whatever moral content the religion supplies and whatever motive the individual brings. Its ethical value is therefore a function of two things: (i) whether the religion's **ultimate is itself moralised** — a God whose nature is goodness, a *dharma* that is justice — and (ii) whether the individual's orientation is **intrinsic** or **extrinsic**.

**[FACT] The objection and its reply.** ⚠️ **Objection:** this makes religiosity morally inert. **Reply:** not inert — an amplifier is not neutral in *effect*, only in *direction*; and traditions can be assessed by how strongly they build internal critique and moral universalism into the amplification.

**[INFERENCE] The directive point.** "Can one claim that there is an inter-relatedness between A and B?" is buying a **conditional** verdict with the condition named. Neither a flat yes nor a flat no scores well. The scoring answer is "yes, under these two conditions", and the two conditions are the moralisation of the ultimate and the orientation of the agent.

**Objection.** The amplifier metaphor is too convenient: it lets religion take credit for every saint and disclaim every fanatic.
**Reply.** **[INFERENCE]** Fair, and the reply must add a constraint rather than repeat the metaphor. The constraint is the first condition of the verdict: traditions differ in whether their *ultimate* is itself moralised, and that is a feature of the religion, not of the individual. A tradition whose ultimate is explicitly a standard of justice cannot disclaim responsibility for conduct performed in its name in the way that a tradition with a purely ritual ultimate might. The metaphor therefore assigns responsibility rather than evading it — provided the first condition is stated.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH

> **Claim:** There is a genuine inter-relatedness between religiosity and immorality, but it is conditional rather than necessary.
> **Named evidence:** The ritual-substitution danger is attested by the traditions' own prophetic critiques — Amos 5 and Isaiah 1, the Buddha on Vedic sacrifice, Kabīr and Nānak on ritualism, the *Gītā* 2.42–43 — while Allport's intrinsic/extrinsic distinction and Batson's quest orientation show that moral correlations differ sharply by orientation, and Kierkegaard's teleological suspension states the override route honestly.
> **Analysis:** Because every tradition contains the critique and every orientation yields different correlations, religiosity behaves as an amplifier of pre-existing moral content and motive rather than as an independent moral cause.
> **Qualification:** The verdict is therefore conditional on two things — whether the religion's ultimate is itself moralised, and whether the agent's orientation is intrinsic or extrinsic — and the comparison class must include secular ideologies, since comparable failures there show religiosity is not the differentiating variable.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ In the 2018 stem, keep the printed word "immorality". Silently regularising it to "morality" changes the question and is visible to an examiner.
2. ⚠️ Do not answer with a flat yes or a flat no. The directive "can one claim that there is an inter-relatedness" buys a *conditional* verdict with the condition named.
3. ⚠️ Do not treat religiosity as a single variable. Without the intrinsic / extrinsic / quest apparatus the answer cannot explain why the correlations conflict.

#### MINI RECAP

Religiosity is the disposition, not the system. Four arguments for: ritual substitution, boundary-marking, suspension, scrupulosity/licensing. Three against: reformers, internal corruption, comparison class. Verdict: an amplifier — conditional on a moralised ultimate and an intrinsic orientation.

---

### SESSION 17 — DEVOTED COMMITMENT, SOCIAL MORALITY AND THE INCOMPATIBILITY CLAIM

#### DEFINITION

**Plain-language definition.** Two printed questions ask the same thing from opposite ends: does a serious religious life pull a person *away* from ordinary social morality, and are religious beliefs and practices *incompatible* with moral behaviour?

**Technical definition.** **[FACT]** The 2019 stem asks whether devoted commitment to a religious way of life makes one go astray from social morality; the 2020 stem asks whether religious beliefs and practices are **incompatible** with moral behaviour. The first is a claim about a **causal or normative tendency** and is answered with the disposition/system distinction and the intrinsic/extrinsic apparatus; the second is an **incompatibility** claim, and a single clear counter-case defeats a necessity claim.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> An incompatibility claim is the easiest kind of claim to state and the hardest to defend, because it is refuted by one clear case — and the history of religious moral reform supplies not one but a class of them.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**incompatibility is a necessity claim · one counter-case defeats it · disposition versus system · withdrawal and world-renunciation · teleological suspension · role conflict · reform movements as counter-cases · internal corruption · orientation-dependence · compatible but not guaranteed**

**How to use them:** the structural sentence is "an incompatibility claim is defeated by a counter-case, whereas a tendency claim requires the conditions to be named". Saying which kind of claim you are facing, and then answering it in the appropriate way, is the whole difference between the two questions.

#### VISUAL — TWO STEMS, TWO LOGICAL SHAPES

```text
   2019 Q5(d): "Does devoted commitment to a religious way of life make man
                go astray from SOCIAL MORALITY? Examine."
   SHAPE: a TENDENCY claim  ⇒ name the conditions under which it holds
   ┌────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │ ROUTES BY WHICH IT COULD   │ WHAT BLOCKS EACH ROUTE                 │
   ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
   │ WITHDRAWAL: renunciation   │ withdrawal is a REORDERING of social    │
   │ removes the agent from     │ duty, not its abandonment; and many    │
   │ ordinary social duty       │ traditions bind the renouncer by       │
   │                            │ stricter, not looser, obligations      │
   ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
   │ SUSPENSION: religious duty │ Kierkegaard CONCEDES the agent cannot  │
   │ overrides ethics           │ make himself intelligible ⇒ no PUBLIC  │
   │ (Kierkegaard)              │ justification is available             │
   ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
   │ BOUNDARY: in-group loyalty │ EXTRINSIC orientation, not commitment  │
   │ displaces impartial duty   │ as such (Allport / Batson)             │
   ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
   │ SUBSTITUTION: observance   │ the PROPHETIC CRITIQUE inside every    │
   │ replaces justice           │ tradition names and condemns this      │
   └────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────┘

   2020 Q8(b): "Are religious beliefs and practices INCOMPATIBLE with moral
                behaviour? Discuss."
   SHAPE: a NECESSITY claim  ⇒ ONE clear counter-case defeats it
   ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │ COUNTER-CASES: reform movements against caste and sati; prophetic     │
   │ justice against ritual; Jain non-injury; Buddhist compassion;         │
   │ the vow strengthened by religious discipline                          │
   │ ⇒ INCOMPATIBILITY IS FALSE                                            │
   │                                                                       │
   │ WHAT SURVIVES THE REFUTATION: the WEAKER claim that the relation is   │
   │ CONTINGENT — religion does not GUARANTEE moral behaviour either.      │
   │ Compatible ≠ guaranteed. Say both.                                    │
   └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

*Identify the logical shape first. A tendency claim needs conditions; a necessity claim needs a counter-case. Answering one in the manner of the other is the commonest structural failure on this pair.*

#### TEACHING

**[FACT] The 2019 stem is a tendency claim.** "Does a devoted commitment to a religious way of life make man go astray from social morality? Examine."
