The directive is buying a *test of a causal or normative claim*, and the structural consequence is to **distinguish the disposition from the system** and use the intrinsic / extrinsic apparatus.

**[FACT] The four routes by which the tendency could operate.** Withdrawal from ordinary social duty; suspension of the ethical in favour of a religious command; boundary-marking that displaces impartial duty toward outsiders; and substitution of observance for justice. Each is a real route, and each has an equally real block, which is why the honest verdict is conditional.

**[FACT] Kierkegaard's concession, used correctly.** The teleological suspension of the ethical is the strongest statement of the suspension route — and it is also its own limit, because Kierkegaard concedes that Abraham **cannot make himself intelligible**. An agent who cannot make himself intelligible cannot offer a *public* justification, so the route exists in the first person and closes in the third.

**[FACT] The 2020 stem is a necessity claim.** "Are religious beliefs and practices incompatible with moral behaviour? Discuss." The directive is buying an **incompatibility claim tested, not a survey**, and the structural consequence is that **one clear counter-case defeats a necessity claim**.

**[FACT] The counter-cases.** Reform movements against caste and *satī*; the prophetic demand for justice against ritual; the Jain vow of non-injury; Buddhist compassion; the canonical example of a vow of non-violence strengthened by religious discipline. Any one of these defeats incompatibility.

**[FACT] What survives the refutation.** ⚠️ The weaker and true claim: the relation is **contingent**. Religion does not *guarantee* moral behaviour any more than it precludes it, and religious narratives and institutions can support care or sanction exclusion. "Compatible" and "guaranteed" are different claims, and a strong answer states both.

**[INFERENCE] Why the two stems belong in one session.** They are the same question in opposite logical dress. The 2019 stem overstates a tendency; the 2020 stem overstates a necessity. Both are answered from the same materials — orientation, internal critique, the prophetic tradition, the reform record — but the *shape* of the answer differs, and an examiner is testing whether the candidate notices that the directive has changed the demand.

**Objection.** The reform counter-cases prove too little: they show that religion *can* accompany moral behaviour, not that religious belief is doing any work. A reformer might simply be a good person who happened to be religious.
**Reply.** **[INFERENCE]** For refuting *incompatibility*, mere accompaniment is enough — that is the logic of a necessity claim, and the objection concedes it. For the stronger claim that religious belief did work, the evidence has to be the *content* of the argument the reformer used: where the appeal is to non-harm, the dignity of persons, the unity of the spiritual and the equality of souls, the moral conclusion is drawn from a religious premise rather than alongside one. That is a limited but genuine answer, and stating its limits is what makes it credible.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH

> **Claim:** Religious beliefs and practices are not incompatible with moral behaviour, and devoted commitment does not systematically lead away from social morality — though both relations are contingent.
> **Named evidence:** Reform movements against caste and *satī*, the prophetic demand for justice over ritual, Jain non-injury and Buddhist compassion are counter-cases sufficient to defeat a necessity claim; and the routes by which commitment might lead astray — withdrawal, Kierkegaardian suspension, boundary-marking and ritual substitution — are each blocked, the suspension route by Kierkegaard's own concession that the agent cannot make himself intelligible.
> **Analysis:** An incompatibility claim is refuted by a single counter-case, whereas a tendency claim requires its conditions to be named, so the two printed stems demand structurally different answers drawn from the same evidence.
> **Qualification:** What survives is the weaker true claim that the relation is contingent in both directions: religion neither precludes nor guarantees moral behaviour, and the operative variable is the agent's orientation and the moral content of the tradition rather than the intensity of commitment.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Do not answer an incompatibility stem with a survey of examples on both sides. Identify it as a necessity claim and defeat it with a counter-case; then state what survives.
2. ⚠️ Do not use Kierkegaard as evidence *for* the suspension route without his concession. The concession that Abraham cannot make himself intelligible is what closes the route to public justification.
3. ⚠️ Do not conclude that religion guarantees moral behaviour after refuting incompatibility. Compatible is not guaranteed, and overshooting here undoes the credit earned by the refutation.

#### MINI RECAP

2019: a tendency claim — name the four routes and the four blocks. 2020: a necessity claim — one counter-case kills it. Both answers end at the same place: the relation is contingent, orientation-dependent, and neither precluded nor guaranteed.

---

### SESSION 18 — RELIGIOUS VIOLENCE: FIVE CANDIDATE ARGUMENTS, TESTED TO THEIR FAILING PREMISES

#### DEFINITION

**Plain-language definition.** The printed question is not "have religions been violent?" — that is history. It is whether any *argument* with religious premises can validly license violence. So the answer must be a set of arguments, each broken at a specific premise.

**Technical definition.** **[FACT]** ⚠️ **Handling rule.** Treat this as a **philosophical question about the structure of justification**, not as a survey of atrocities, and not as a comment on any living community. Assess arguments; name no contemporary group. **[FACT]** The exact question is: *can a philosophical argument support violence in the name of religion?* — that is, is there a valid argument with religious premises whose conclusion licenses violence?

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> There is no **sound** philosophical argument for violence in the name of religion: the valid-looking candidates each fail at a premise — the identification of the divine will, the vulnerability of the sacred, the efficacy of coercion, or the total evil of the adversary — and what survives is the ordinary permission of defensive force, which is not religious at all.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**structure of justification, not history · valid but unsound · failing premise · infallible identification of the divine will · Kierkegaard's teleological suspension · public justification · value versus vulnerability · Locke, *A Letter Concerning Toleration* (1689) · belief is not directly voluntary · *compelle intrare* (Luke 14:23) examined, not endorsed · cosmic dualism · total demonisation · identity-honour is not a religious argument · Euthyphro · epistemic fallibility · Gandhi's means–end unity · internal critique · restraint, not licence**

**How to use them:** the words "valid in form, unsound at premise P2" must appear. They are the difference between an essay about religion and violence and an answer to the question that was actually printed.

#### VISUAL — FIVE ARGUMENTS, FIVE BROKEN PREMISES

*Five rows, five broken premises, four general rebuttals, one survivor. This table is the answer; the prose around it is commentary.*

#### TEACHING

**[FACT] The exact question.** Not "have religions been violent?" — a historical question — but "**can a philosophical argument support violence in the name of religion?**". That is a question about whether a valid argument with religious premises has a conclusion that licenses violence.

**[FACT] Argument one, divine command.** *P1:* Whatever God commands is obligatory. *P2:* God has commanded this act. *C:* The act is obligatory, whatever its ordinary moral appearance. **Test:** the argument is *formally valid*; it fails on **P2**, which requires an infallible identification of the divine will that no fallible agent possesses. Kierkegaard's teleological suspension concedes exactly this: Abraham cannot make himself intelligible, and therefore cannot make a *public* case. An argument that cannot be publicly assessed cannot function as a justification to others.

**[FACT] Argument two, defence of the sacred.** *P1:* The sacred is of supreme value. *P2:* Supreme value may be defended by force. *C:* Violence in defence of the sacred is permitted. **Test:** P2 conflates *value* with *vulnerability*. Persons can be defended by force because they can be harmed; the sacred, on most theologies, cannot be diminished by an insult, so the premise misdescribes what is at stake.

**[FACT] Argument three, soteriological paternalism.** *P1:* Eternal salvation outweighs temporal harm. *P2:* Coercion can produce conformity that leads to salvation. *C:* Coercion is a benefit. **Test:** **P2 is false**, and this is the decisive philosophical refutation. **Locke's *A Letter Concerning Toleration* (1689):** the magistrate's force can compel outward profession but "the understanding cannot be compelled to the belief of anything by outward force"; belief is not directly voluntary, so coerced religion produces hypocrisy, not salvation. ✅ Augustine's use of *compelle intrare* (Luke 14:23) in the Donatist controversy is the standard historical counter-instance, and it is a **historical position to be examined**, not an endorsement.

**[FACT] Argument four, cosmic dualism.** *P1:* History is a war between absolute good and absolute evil. *P2:* Opponents instantiate absolute evil. *C:* Restraint toward them is complicity. **Test:** **P2 is the flaw** — the total demonisation of persons is precisely what most religious anthropologies deny: the image of God, the Buddha-nature, the self (*ātman*) in all beings, non-injury (*ahiṃsā*).

**[FACT] Argument five, identity and honour.** **Test:** this is not a religious argument at all but a **group-identity** argument wearing religious vocabulary; it is refuted by the ordinary objections to collective punishment.

**[FACT] The four general rebuttals.** (a) **Euthyphro**: if the act is wrong, calling it commanded does not make it right; if it is right, the command is not what makes it so. (b) **Epistemic fallibility**: revelation-claims are contested even inside a tradition, so no agent is entitled to the certainty the argument requires. (c) **Means–end unity**: Gandhi's insistence that means and ends are inseparable — "the means may be likened to a seed, the end to a tree" — denies that a sacred end can sanctify an unholy means. (d) **Internal critique**: every major tradition contains its own resources against violence — non-injury (*ahiṃsā*), the Buddhist first precept, the prophetic critique of sacrifice, and the doctrine of the dignity of the person.

**[FACT] What survives.** ⚠️ **Only the ordinary, non-religious justification of defensive force** — self-defence and the protection of the innocent — survives, and it is available to a secular ethic as well. **Religion adds no independent licence.** What religion *can* legitimately add is a **restraint**: many traditions impose limits on defensive force stricter than secular law requires.

**[INFERENCE] Why validity must be conceded before soundness is denied.** A script that says "these arguments are illogical" has misdescribed them and will be marked down. Arguments one to four are *valid*: their conclusions do follow from their premises. That is exactly what makes them dangerous and exactly why the philosophical work is at the premises. Conceding validity and then locating the false premise is the move that demonstrates philosophical competence rather than moral disapproval.

**Objection.** Locating a false premise is too easy a victory. A determined believer will simply insist that P2 is true in his case — that *he* has correctly identified the divine will — and the philosopher has no way to disprove it.
**Reply.** **[INFERENCE]** The reply is not that P2 can be disproved but that it cannot be *publicly established*, and public establishability is what a justification requires. Kierkegaard's concession is decisive precisely because it comes from a defender of the suspension: the agent cannot make himself intelligible. A premise that is in principle unavailable to assessment by anyone but the agent can ground a first-person conviction and cannot ground a justification offered to others — which is the only thing the printed question asks about.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH

> **Claim:** No sound philosophical argument supports violence in the name of religion, although several such arguments are formally valid.
> **Named evidence:** The divine-command argument fails at the premise of infallible identification, as Kierkegaard's own concession that Abraham cannot make himself intelligible shows; the defence-of-the-sacred argument conflates value with vulnerability; soteriological paternalism is refuted by Locke's demonstration in *A Letter Concerning Toleration* (1689) that belief cannot be compelled by outward force; and cosmic dualism contradicts the image of God, the Buddha-nature, the self in all beings and non-injury.
> **Analysis:** Because each argument is valid and unsound, the philosophical work lies wholly at the premises, and the identity-honour case is not even a religious argument but a group-identity claim in religious vocabulary.
> **Qualification:** What survives is the ordinary permission of defensive force, which is available to any secular ethic and is therefore not a religious licence at all; religion's distinctive contribution here is restraint, since many traditions impose stricter limits than secular law requires.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ **Do not answer the religious-violence question with a list of conflicts.** The stem asks whether a *philosophical argument* can support it — so state candidate arguments in premises and show **which premise fails**.
2. ⚠️ Do not characterise any living community and do not adjudicate between faiths. Assess arguments; name no contemporary group.
3. ⚠️ Do not present Augustine's *compelle intrare* as an endorsement. It is a historical position examined as a counter-instance to Locke, and mishandling it is both a factual and a tonal error.

#### MINI RECAP

Structure, not history. Five candidates: command, sacred, coercion, dualism, identity. Four broken premises plus one non-religious impostor. Four general rebuttals: Euthyphro, fallibility, means–end unity, internal critique. Survivor: ordinary defensive force. Religion's contribution: restraint, not licence.

---

### SESSION 19 — RESTRAINT-REGIMES COMPARED: JUST WAR, *JIHĀD* AND *DHARMA-YUDDHA*

#### DEFINITION

**Plain-language definition.** Three traditions independently produced rules limiting who may fight, for what, and how. Compared as *restraint systems* rather than as licences, they converge — and each also contains a realist counter-tradition that relaxes the rules under necessity.

**Technical definition.** **[FACT]** ⚠️ **Handling rule (non-negotiable).** These are **internally contested normative traditions with long juristic histories**. Compare their *criteria*; do not adjudicate between faiths, do not characterise any community, and mark contested points as contested. The examinable content is the **structure of restraint**, which is where the three converge.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> The three frameworks are best compared as competing **restraint-regimes**, not as licences: their convergence on authority, cause, intention, discrimination and proportionality suggests these are conclusions of practical reason that each tradition articulates in its own idiom, while their internal realist counter-currents show that no tradition has fully solved the problem of necessity.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**restraint-regime, not licence · *jus ad bellum* · *jus in bello* · *jus post bellum* · legitimate authority (*auctoritas principis*) · just cause (*causa iusta*) · right intention (*intentio recta*) · Aquinas, *ST* II-II q.40 · Michael Walzer, *Just and Unjust Wars* (1977) · discrimination · proportionality · *jihād* as striving · *qitāl* · greater/lesser *jihād* ❓ disputed · *dār al-islām*/*dār al-ḥarb* as juristic constructs · *dharma-yuddha* · *sāma, dāna, bheda, daṇḍa* · Kauṭilya's *kūṭa-yuddha* · internal realist counter-tradition**

**How to use them:** the phrase that earns the mark is "competing restraint-regimes". It reframes the entire comparison away from the question "which religion permits violence?" — which no philosophy answer should be attempting — and toward the question the syllabus actually supports.

#### VISUAL — CONVERGENCE, DIVERGENCE AND THE COUNTER-TRADITIONS

```text
  │                     │           │ juristic conditions│ BEFORE danda   │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────┼────────────────────┼────────────────┤
  │ Non-combatant       │ CENTRAL   │ EXPLICIT           │ DETAILED lists │
  │ immunity            │           │ prohibitions       │ of the protected│
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────┼────────────────────┼────────────────┤
  │ Proportionality of  │ YES       │ YES                │ YES (prohibited│
  │ means               │           │                    │ weapons)       │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────┼────────────────────┼────────────────┤
  │ Temporal/spatial    │ not       │ truce and treaty   │ cease at       │
  │ limits              │ classical │ rules              │ sunset; fight  │
  │                     │           │                    │ only equals    │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────┼────────────────────┼────────────────┤
  │ INTERNAL REALIST    │ realism,  │ contested modern   │ KAUTILYA's     │
  │ COUNTER-TRADITION   │ raison    │ readings           │ KUTA-YUDDHA    │
  │                     │ d'etat    │                    │                │
  └─────────────────────┴───────────┴────────────────────┴────────────────┘

  THE PHILOSOPHICAL YIELD
  Three independent traditions converge on AUTHORITY, CAUSE, INTENTION,
  DISCRIMINATION and PROPORTIONALITY. That convergence is evidence that the
  constraints are RATIONALLY DERIVABLE rather than revelation-dependent —
  which cuts AGAINST the claim that religion supplies a special licence and
  FOR the claim that religion supplies culturally embedded RESTRAINT.
  Every one of the three ALSO contains an internal counter-tradition that
  relaxes the constraints under necessity: that is the honest qualification.

  CONTESTED ITEMS THAT MUST BE FLAGGED, NOT ASSERTED
  ❓ "greater / lesser jihad": rests on a report whose authenticity is
     DISPUTED by hadith scholars — cite WITH the flag, never as canonical.
  ⚠ dar al-islam / dar al-harb: JURISTIC CONSTRUCTIONS of a particular
     historical period, NOT Qur'anic terms; contemporary applicability is
     contested WITHIN the tradition.
  ⚠ modern militant reinterpretations that drop authority-conditions and
     non-combatant immunity are CONTESTED FROM WITHIN by mainstream jurists.
  ⚠ the Gita's justification of Arjuna's fighting is a SVADHARMA argument
     internal to a narrative situation; Gandhi's allegorical reading (the
     battlefield as the human heart) is ONE influential interpretation among
     several, not the plain sense.
```

*The matrix is the comparison; the yield paragraph is the philosophical point; the flagged block is what protects the answer from asserting contested material as settled.*

#### TEACHING

**[FACT] Just war.** Augustine and Aquinas supply the classical core; Aquinas' three conditions (*Summa Theologiae* II-II q.40) are **legitimate authority (*auctoritas principis*)**, **just cause (*causa iusta*)** and **right intention (*intentio recta*)**. The later tradition, systematised in modern form by **Michael Walzer** (*Just and Unjust Wars*, 1977), separates ***jus ad bellum*** — just cause, competent authority, right intention, proportionality of ends, last resort, reasonable prospect of success and the aim of a just peace — from ***jus in bello*** — **discrimination** (non-combatant immunity) and **proportionality** of means; later writers add ***jus post bellum***.

**[FACT] The classical Islamic juristic tradition.** *Jihād* means **striving or exertion**; the classical juristic literature treats armed struggle under the distinct term ***qitāl*** (fighting), hedged by conditions: proper authority, just cause, and restraints the jurists derive from prophetic practice — **non-combatants (women, children, the aged, the clergy) are not to be targeted**, crops and trees are not to be wantonly destroyed, treaties are to be honoured, and mutilation is forbidden.

**[FACT] Three contested items in that tradition, to be flagged rather than asserted.** ❓ The widely cited distinction between the "**greater** *jihād*" (the inner struggle against the self, *jihād al-nafs*) and the "**lesser** *jihād*" (outward fighting) rests on a report whose authenticity is **disputed by ḥadīth scholars** — cite it, but flag the dispute rather than asserting it as canonical. ⚠️ The territorial categories *dār al-islām*/*dār al-ḥarb* are **juristic constructions of a particular historical period**, not Qur'ānic terms, and their contemporary applicability is contested within the tradition. ⚠️ Modern militant reinterpretations that dispense with authority-conditions and non-combatant immunity are **contested from within** the tradition by mainstream jurists; a philosophy answer should note the internal contestation rather than treating any one reading as "the" doctrine.

**[FACT] *Dharma-yuddha*.** The *Mahābhārata* — especially the *Śānti-* and *Anuśāsana-parvans* — and the Dharmaśāstra literature set out rules of righteous war: war only after the graded political means (*sāma, dāna, bheda, daṇḍa*) are exhausted; **do not strike** one who is asleep, unarmed, fleeing, surrendering, wounded, disarmed, a non-combatant, or engaged with another; fight only an equal; hostilities cease at sunset; poisoned and barbed weapons are forbidden; envoys, physicians, farmers and their property are protected.

**[FACT] The internal tension is the examinable point.** ⚠️ The epic itself narrates repeated violations, and **Kauṭilya's *Arthaśāstra*** explicitly countenances ***kūṭa-yuddha*** — concealed or stratagem warfare — on grounds of state-interest, against the epic's ***dharma-yuddha*** ideal. The Indian tradition therefore contains its own realist counter-tradition, and a good answer names it.

**[FACT] The *Gītā* caution.** ⚠️ The *Gītā*'s justification of Arjuna's fighting is a **role-duty (*svadharma*)** argument internal to a specific narrative situation; Gandhi's allegorical reading — the battlefield as the human heart — is one influential interpretation among several, not the plain sense.

**[FACT] The philosophical yield.** ⚠️ Three independent traditions converge on **authority, cause, intention, discrimination and proportionality**. That convergence is evidence that the constraints are **rationally derivable** rather than revelation-dependent — which cuts *against* the claim that religion supplies a special licence and *for* the claim that religion supplies culturally embedded restraint. ⚠️ Every one of the three also contains an internal counter-tradition that relaxes the constraints under necessity, which is the honest qualification.

**[INFERENCE] Why this session belongs to a philosophy paper at all.** Because the convergence is an *argument*. If three traditions with different scriptures, different metaphysics and different histories arrive independently at the same five constraints, the best explanation is that the constraints are conclusions of practical reason articulated in three idioms — which is a substantive philosophical claim about the relation between religion and morality, and it is the reason this comparison is owned by this clause rather than by history.

**Objection.** Comparing restraint-regimes is a way of flattering all three traditions. The rules were routinely violated, and a normative framework whose violation is the norm is not evidence of restraint but of rhetoric.
**Reply.** **[INFERENCE]** The premise must be conceded without hedging — the *Mahābhārata* itself narrates the violations, and the comparison would be dishonest if it suppressed them. But the philosophical claim being made is about the *content of the norms*, not their compliance rate. That three traditions independently specify authority, cause, intention, discrimination and proportionality is evidence about what practical reason converges on; and each tradition's own record of violation and its own realist counter-tradition are precisely why the qualification "no tradition has fully solved the problem of necessity" is stated as part of the verdict rather than added under pressure.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH

> **Claim:** Just war, the classical Islamic juristic tradition and *dharma-yuddha* are best compared as competing restraint-regimes rather than as licences for violence.
> **Named evidence:** Aquinas at *ST* II-II q.40 requires legitimate authority, just cause and right intention, and Walzer's *Just and Unjust Wars* (1977) systematises discrimination and proportionality; the classical juristic literature places armed struggle under *qitāl* with non-combatant protections and treaty obligations; and the *Mahābhārata* and Dharmaśāstra require the graded means *sāma, dāna, bheda, daṇḍa* before force and protect the sleeping, fleeing, wounded and disarmed.
> **Analysis:** Because three traditions with different scriptures converge on authority, cause, intention, discrimination and proportionality, the best explanation is that these constraints are conclusions of practical reason expressed in three idioms, which cuts against any claim that religion supplies a distinctive licence.
> **Qualification:** Each tradition also carries an internal realist counter-current — *raison d'état*, contested modern readings, and Kauṭilya's *kūṭa-yuddha* — so none has fully solved the problem of necessity, and contested items such as the greater/lesser *jihād* report and the *dār al-islām*/*dār al-ḥarb* categories must be flagged rather than asserted.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Do not characterise any living community and do not adjudicate between faiths. Compare **criteria**, mark contested items, and name the internal counter-traditions.
2. ❓ Do not assert the "greater/lesser *jihād*" distinction as canonical. Its supporting report is disputed by ḥadīth scholars — cite it **with** the flag.
3. ⚠️ Do not treat *dār al-islām*/*dār al-ḥarb* as Qur'ānic, and do not read the *Gītā* as a general theory of war. Both errors substitute a convenient shorthand for a contested juristic or interpretive history.

#### MINI RECAP

Restraint-regimes, not licences. Five convergent criteria: authority, cause, intention, discrimination, proportionality. Three counter-traditions: *raison d'état*, contested modern readings, *kūṭa-yuddha*. Flag the disputed report and the juristic constructs. Yield: the constraints look rationally derivable.

### SESSION 20 — NIETZSCHE'S CRITICISM OF RELIGION *AND* MORALITY

#### DEFINITION

**Plain-language definition.** Nietzsche's target is not only God. It is the morality that the belief in God carried — and his claim is that when the belief goes, the morality has lost its foundation without anyone noticing.

**Technical definition.** ✅ **Ownership note.** The 2025 stem couples *religion* with *morality*, so this file is the primary owner and carries the full dossier; the Religion without God owner retains Nietzsche only as a critique of theism and as the bridge to post-theistic value, and routes the printed question here. **[FACT]** Nietzsche's critique has **two inseparable halves**: a diagnosis of the collapse of the theistic-metaphysical worldview ("God is dead"), and a **genealogy** of the morality that worldview underwrote, which he judges to be life-denying.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Nietzsche's achievement is to make **morality itself** an object of historical and psychological suspicion, and to show that the death of God is a problem for ethics before it is a problem for theology; his failure is the constructive half, since the revaluation supplies no criterion by which one creation of values is better than another — which is why his critique is more durable than his solution.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**"God is dead" as cultural diagnosis, not atheological argument · *The Gay Science* §125, §108, §343 · *On the Genealogy of Morality* (1887) · good/bad versus good/evil · slave revolt in morality · *ressentiment* as creative and reactive · guilt (*Schuld*) from debt (*Schulden*) · bad conscience · infinite creditor · the ascetic ideal · "rather will nothingness than not will" · the will to truth destroys the belief in God · "Platonism for the people" (*BGE* Preface) · pity (*Mitleid*) · revaluation of all values · will to power · Übermensch · eternal recurrence · *amor fati* · nihilism as the interim · genetic fallacy · Max Scheler, *Ressentiment* (1912)**

**How to use them:** the stem says "religion **and** morality". The examiner is buying **both objects and the link between them**. Structure the answer so that the genealogy is presented as the consequence of the diagnosis, and say so explicitly in a transition sentence.

#### VISUAL — THE TWO HALVES, THE THREE ESSAYS, AND THE FOUR OBJECTIONS

*Two halves, one link. Three essays in sequence. Four objections with named replies. Three Indian comparisons, each with its precise limit. This is the fullest single diagram in the package because the 2025 question is the fullest single demand.*

#### TEACHING

**[FACT] The two inseparable halves.** A diagnosis of the collapse of the theistic-metaphysical worldview, and a genealogy of the morality that worldview underwrote. The stem couples the two objects, so the *link* between them is itself part of the answer: the morality is left standing on a foundation that has been removed.

**[FACT] "God is dead."** *The Gay Science* §125 — the madman announces that we have killed God and asks who will wipe the blood off us. ⚠️ **Read it correctly:** it is a **cultural diagnosis**, not an atheological argument. The claim is that the Christian-metaphysical framework has become *unbelievable* to the very culture built on it, and that the culture has not yet grasped the consequences. §108 and §343 make the delayed-consequences point explicitly.

**[FACT] First essay — "Good and Evil", "Good and Bad".** The original aristocratic valuation is **good/bad**: "good" names the noble, strong, affirming type, and "bad" is merely the contrasting low. The **slave revolt in morality** begins when *ressentiment* — the impotent resentment of those who cannot act — becomes creative and produces values. It inverts the scheme: the noble is renamed **evil**, and the powerless, meek and abstinent become **good**. The decisive point is that slave morality is **reactive**: it says No to an outside first, and derives its Yes from that No.

**[FACT] Second essay — guilt and bad conscience.** Nietzsche derives moral guilt (*Schuld*) from the economic relation of debt (*Schulden*); when the outward discharge of instinct is blocked by society, cruelty turns inward and produces the "bad conscience"; religion then maximises this by positing an infinite creditor who cannot be repaid.

**[FACT] Third essay — the ascetic ideal.** The ascetic ideal gave suffering a *meaning*, which is why it prevailed: "man would rather will nothingness than not will". Its final irony is that modern **science and the will to truth** are themselves the ascetic ideal's latest form — and it is that unconditional will to truth which finally destroys the belief in God.

**[FACT] The critique of Christianity specifically.** *Beyond Good and Evil*, Preface: Christianity is "**Platonism for the people**" — the transfer of a two-world metaphysics to the masses. In *The Antichrist* he attacks **pity (*Mitleid*)** as a multiplier of suffering and a preserver of the ill-constituted, and treats the church as an inversion of the values that make life ascend. ⚠️ Use paraphrase; his rhetoric is extreme and misquotation is easy.

**[FACT] The positive programme.** **Revaluation of all values**; the **will to power** as the interpretive principle of valuation; the **Übermensch** of *Thus Spoke Zarathustra* as the type who creates values rather than receiving them; **eternal recurrence** as the ultimate test of affirmation; ***amor fati***. **Nihilism** is the interim condition between the collapse of the old values and the creation of new ones — Nietzsche is its diagnostician and its opponent, not its advocate.

**[FACT] The presupposition.** ⚠️ That valuations have **histories** traceable to types of life, and that they can be assessed as *life-enhancing* or *life-denying* — which presupposes a normative standard ("life", "ascending life") that Nietzsche never systematically justifies.

**[FACT] Four objections with their replies.** (1) **Genetic fallacy.** Showing that a value *originated* in resentment does not show it is false. *Reply:* his target is not truth-value but **value** — what kind of life a valuation expresses and produces, a distinct and non-fallacious question, though it does not deliver the refutation his rhetoric suggests. (2) **Missing criterion.** If all values are created, on what basis is the Übermensch's creation *better*? *Reply:* the standard is internal — self-overcoming, affirmation, the capacity to will one's life eternally — but critics reply that this is a preference, not a norm. (3) **Caricature of Christian ethics.** Agapeic love, prophetic justice and the dignity of the person are not reducible to *ressentiment*; **Max Scheler** (*Ressentiment*, 1912) argues directly that Christian love descends from abundance, not from impotence — the single best-named counter-authority to cite. (4) **Political appropriation.** The doctrine is vulnerable to elitist and worse readings — a vulnerability that is partly a feature of Nietzsche's own rhetoric, even though his sister's editorial role in the posthumous material is well documented.

**[FACT] The Indian comparison.** ⚠️ The *Gītā*'s opening is structurally the same accusation: Kṛṣṇa charges Arjuna with *klaibya* — unmanliness or impotence (2.3) — and calls him to act. But the resemblance stops there: the *Gītā*'s remedy is **role-duty (*svadharma*) and non-attachment**, a *duty*-ethic grounded in cosmic order, not a self-legislated creation of values. Nietzsche's charge that Buddhism is "life-denying" is a real and repeated claim of his, and is standardly answered: *nirvāṇa* is the cessation of *craving*, not of life; compassion (*karuṇā*) is an active, other-regarding energy; and the Buddha's middle way explicitly rejects self-mortification, which is precisely the ascetic ideal Nietzsche attacks. ⚠️ Nietzsche in fact rates Buddhism above Christianity as "more realistic" while still classing it as nihilistic — say this precisely rather than flattening it. **Cārvāka** is the closest Indian analogue in *conclusion* — this-worldly value, rejection of the priestly order — but not in *method*: it is empiricist where Nietzsche is genealogical.

**[INFERENCE] Why the ownership routing matters in the exam.** A candidate who has revised Nietzsche only as a critic of theism will produce half the answer and miss the marks in the genealogy. The coupling in the printed stem — *religion* **and** *morality* — is the instruction to supply both halves and their connection, which is exactly why this clause, and not the Religion without God clause, is the primary owner.

**Objection.** If Nietzsche's standard is "life" and he never justifies it, then his critique is as groundless as the morality he attacks, and the whole genealogy is rhetoric.
**Reply.** **[INFERENCE]** The premise is conceded — it is stated in the dossier as the unjustified presupposition, and an answer should state it too. But the conclusion overshoots. A critique can be *diagnostically* successful without being foundationally justified: showing that a valuation has a history, a psychology and a function is a real result about that valuation, and it stands whether or not the critic's own standard is secure. That is precisely why the verdict formula says his critique is more durable than his solution rather than saying both survive or neither does.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH

> **Claim:** Nietzsche's criticism of religion and of morality is one connected argument: the collapse of the theistic-metaphysical worldview leaves the morality it carried without a foundation.
> **Named evidence:** *The Gay Science* §125 announces the death of God as a cultural diagnosis, with §108 and §343 making the delayed-consequences point; the three essays of the *Genealogy* (1887) then derive good/evil from the slave revolt and *ressentiment*, guilt from debt and the bad conscience, and the prevalence of the ascetic ideal from its gift of meaning to suffering, ending in the irony that the will to truth destroys the belief in God.
> **Analysis:** Because the genealogy explains the morality as a product of the framework whose collapse he has just diagnosed, the two halves are not two topics but one argument, and this is exactly what the coupled stem is testing.
> **Qualification:** The critique faces the genetic-fallacy charge, answered by distinguishing truth-value from value; it lacks a criterion for preferring one creation of values to another; and Scheler's *Ressentiment* (1912) supplies the strongest named counter-authority, arguing that Christian love descends from abundance rather than impotence.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Do not treat Nietzsche's genealogy as a refutation. Origin does not settle validity; his target is the *value* of the valuation, and Scheler is the named counter-authority.
2. ⚠️ Do not read "God is dead" as an argument against God's existence. It is a cultural diagnosis with a delayed-consequences claim, and misreading it costs the whole first half.
3. ⚠️ Do not flatten Nietzsche's view of Buddhism. He rates it above Christianity as more realistic while still classing it as nihilistic, and the correction — that *nirvāṇa* is the cessation of craving, not of life — must be stated precisely.

#### MINI RECAP

Two halves, one link. §125 as diagnosis. Essay I: slave revolt and *ressentiment*, reactive values. Essay II: debt, bad conscience, infinite creditor. Essay III: the ascetic ideal, and the will to truth turning on itself. Programme: revaluation, will to power, Übermensch, recurrence, *amor fati*. Four objections, Scheler named. Indian parity: *klaibya* is not revaluation; *nirvāṇa* is not life-denial.

### SESSION 21 — SYNTHESIS: THE BENCH, THE DECODER, THE LADDER AND THE ANSWER SPINE

#### DEFINITION

**Plain-language definition.** This session is the assembly bay. Everything taught so far is arranged into the four instruments you actually use in the hall: a bench that compares every grounding, a decoder that tells you what each directive is buying, a ladder that tells you what band your script is in, and three answer architectures.

**Technical definition.** **[FACT]** The synthesis carries the **five-row comparison grid**, the **nine-row parity bench** of groundings, the **nine-row inter-thinker debate grid**, the **four standing criticisms with replies**, the **ten-row directive decoder**, the **five-band verdict ladder** with three graded templates, the **three answer architectures** for 10, 15 and 20 marks, the **fifteen common traps**, and the **quotation-discipline table**.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> The Euthyphro shows morality **cannot be wholly reducible to** divine command, so morality has autonomy; yet religion powerfully **motivates and sustains** morality, and morality **purifies** religion — hence the **interaction** view, not strict dependence, is soundest.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**divine command · modified command theory (Adams) · divine motivation theory (Zagzebski) · Alston's standard reply · Euthyphro · arbitrariness · autonomy · heteronomy · categorical imperative · universal law · humanity as end · natural law · moral realism · justification versus motivation · Ring of Gyges · Hobbes's Foole · sensible knave · Bradley's "why should I be moral?" · constrained maximisation · eudaimonism · moral licensing · scrupulosity · intrinsic/extrinsic/quest religiosity · prophetic critique · teleological suspension of the ethical · *jus ad bellum* / *jus in bello* / *jus post bellum* · discrimination · proportionality · legitimate authority · *qitāl* · *jihād al-nafs* ❓ · *dharma-yuddha* · *kūṭa-yuddha* · *sāma-dāna-bheda-daṇḍa* · *compelle intrare* · Locke on the incoercibility of belief · *dharma* · *ṛta* · karma · *ahiṃsā* · *aṇuvrata*/*mahāvrata* · *anekāntavāda* as intellectual non-violence · *cetanā* · *brahmavihāra* · ownerless suffering (Śāntideva) · *niṣkāma-karma* · *codanā* · slave morality · *ressentiment* · ascetic ideal · bad conscience · revaluation · Übermensch · *amor fati* · eternal recurrence · nihilism**

**How to use them:** four safe formulations carry an unusual amount of weight and can be adapted to almost any stem in this clause. "God-reference may strengthen motivation without constituting obligation." · "Religion and morality are conceptually distinguishable, historically interactive and normatively mutually critical." · "Religion's distinctive contribution to the ethics of force is restraint, not licence." · "Religiosity is an amplifier: it magnifies the moral content it is attached to."

#### TEACHING

**[FACT] The four standing criticisms and their replies.**
1. **Euthyphro.** Arbitrary command or independent good. **Reply:** necessary divine goodness. **Rejoinder:** the normativity of goodness still needs explanation.
2. **Motivation gap.** Secular reason may identify duty but fail to motivate. **Reply:** moral motivation can arise from empathy, identity and respect; religion is one source, not a logically necessary one.
3. **Heteronomy.** Reward and punishment corrupt moral worth. **Reply:** mature theism motivates through love of the good, not prudential fear.
4. **Immoral religion.** A religion may preserve rituals while sanctioning injustice. **Reply:** if morality is partly constitutive of worthy religion, such practice is internal corruption rather than a counterexample to ethical religion.

**[FACT] The directive decoder — what each printed directive is buying.**

| Directive | Examiner is buying | Structural consequence | Live example |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Can one claim that there is an inter-relatedness between A and B?** | a **conditional** verdict, with the condition named | neither "yes" nor "no": say *under what conditions* | 2018 Q5(d) religiosity/immorality |
| **Can there be a philosophical argument to support X?** | candidate arguments **in premises**, then the failing premise | never a historical survey | 2019 Q5(c) |
| **Does X make man go astray from Y? Examine** | a test of a causal or normative claim | distinguish the disposition from the system; use intrinsic/extrinsic | 2019 Q5(d) |
| **[Claim]. Examine** | fidelity to the claim's exact scope | "all the time" is the operative phrase — build the answer on it | 2019 Q7(b) |
| **Are A and B incompatible? Discuss** | an incompatibility claim tested, not a survey | one clear counter-case defeats a *necessity* claim | 2020 Q8(b) |
| **Do you consider that A and B are inseparable? Give reasons** | a first-person verdict with ranked reasons | separability must be shown by an actual system, not asserted | 2021 Q6(c) |
| **"Quotation." Discuss** | the quotation's **asymmetry** analysed in both directions | test each direction separately | 2022 Q5(b) |
| **Does A influence B? Explain the interactive relation** | two deliverables — influence **and** the interaction model | causal influence ≠ logical dependence | 2023 Q5(c) |
| **Present an account of X's criticism of A and B** | both objects, and their connection in X's system | Nietzsche's two halves are one argument — show the link | 2025 Q5(c) |
| **Is it necessary for X to bear reference to Y…? Critically discuss** | a **necessity** claim tested | distinguish grounding, epistemic access and motivation | 2025 Q8(b) |

> 🔑 **Rule:** in this clause almost every stem is a claim of **necessity, dependence or incompatibility**. Your first move is always to ask *which* relation is asserted — conceptual dependence, epistemic dependence, motivational dependence, or sociological correlation — because they have four different answers.

**[FACT] The graded verdict ladder.**

| Band | What the script contains | Typical fault |
|---|---|---|
| **Weak (≈40%)** | "religion teaches morality"; divine command and Kant named | the four kinds of dependence merged |
| **Average (≈50%)** | Euthyphro stated with both horns; Kantian autonomy | Indian material limited to one line on *dharma* |
| **Good (≈60%)** | grounding versus motivation separated; one objection answered | modified divine command absent; non-theistic groundings asserted, not argued |
| **Strong (≈65–70%)** | modified divine command and its residual objection; a real non-theistic grounding argued (Śāntideva, Mīmāṃsā); interaction analysed | verdict summarises rather than decides |
| **Top (≈70%+)** | all the above, plus a discriminating distinction — semantic/metaphysical/motivational dependence; intrinsic versus extrinsic religiosity; justification versus motivation; restraint versus licence — plus the concession that costs the writer's own position something | — |

**[FACT] The three graded-verdict templates.**
- ⚠️ *Dependence stem:* "Morality is not **conceptually** dependent on religion — the Euthyphro shows that, and four independent non-theistic groundings confirm it. It may be **motivationally** and **historically** dependent for many agents, and those are different claims that a good answer keeps apart."
- ⚠️ *Asymmetry stem:* "The asymmetry holds, but for a reason the slogan hides: morality is *constitutive* of what we are willing to call a religion, whereas religion is at most *supportive* of morality. Constitutive and supportive relations are not two degrees of one relation."
- ⚠️ *Violence stem:* "No sound argument survives. Each candidate is valid in form and fails at a premise — the identification of the divine will, the vulnerability of the sacred, the efficacy of coercion, or the total evil of the adversary."

**[FACT] The three answer architectures.**

```text
  10 MARKS  (≈150 words)
  state the dependence claim → Euthyphro/Kant discriminator → ONE Indian
  illustration → distinguish grounding from motivation → verdict.

  15 MARKS  (≈250 words)
  frame the obligation problem → strongest theistic account → autonomy or
  natural-law rival → objection and reply → interaction analysis →
  defended conclusion.

  20 MARKS  (≈350 words)
  separate rightness, obligation and motivation → compare command, autonomy
  and dharma → Euthyphro PLUS the immoral-religion objection → replies →
  graded relational thesis.
```

**[FACT] The fifteen common traps, assembled.**
1. Do not use the sentence commonly paraphrased as "without God everything is permitted" as a verified direct quotation from Dostoevsky.
2. Do not reduce the Euthyphro to "God versus morality"; specify both horns.
3. Do not equate autonomy with moral subjectivism.
4. Do not infer logical dependence from sociological influence.
5. Do not identify *dharma* wholly with universal morality.
6. In "religion without morality," distinguish descriptive institutions from normatively adequate religion.
7. Do not answer the religious-violence question with a list of conflicts; state candidate arguments in premises and show which premise fails.
8. Do not characterise any living community, and do not adjudicate between faiths, when handling just war / *jihād* / *dharma-yuddha*; compare criteria, mark contested items, and name the internal counter-traditions.
9. Do not assert the "greater/lesser *jihād*" distinction as canonical; its supporting report is disputed by ḥadīth scholars.
10. Do not treat *dār al-islām*/*dār al-ḥarb* as Qur'ānic; they are juristic-historical constructs whose contemporary application is contested within the tradition.
11. Do not read the *Gītā* as a general theory of war; Kṛṣṇa's argument is *svadharma* within a narrative situation, and Gandhi's allegorical reading is one influential interpretation, not the plain sense.
12. In the 2018 "religiosity and immorality" stem, keep the printed word and use the intrinsic/extrinsic/quest apparatus; the verdict is *conditional ambivalence*, not a correlation.
13. In "why be moral", separate justification from motivation — the printed claim is weak on the first and strong on the second, and saying so is the answer.
14. Do not present modified divine command theory as defeating the Euthyphro outright; it blocks arbitrariness but concedes that "loving/good" is a concept we grasp independently.
15. Do not treat Nietzsche's genealogy as a refutation; origin does not settle validity, his target is the *value* of the valuation, and Scheler's *Ressentiment* is the named counter-authority.

**[FACT] The quotation and attribution discipline table.**

| Item | Status | Safe use |
|---|---|---|
| Euthyphro dilemma | ✅ Plato, *Euthyphro* 10a | state **both** horns |
| "If God does not exist, everything is permitted" | ❓ a summary of a Dostoevskian theme, **not** a verified verbatim line | never present as a quotation |
| *Finite and Infinite Goods* | ✅ Robert Merrihew Adams (1999) | modified divine command: obligation from a loving God's commands; goodness from resemblance to God |
| the "standard metre" reply to Euthyphro | ✅ William Alston | God as the supreme standard, not a conformer |
| "Does Moral Philosophy Rest on a Mistake?" | ✅ H. A. Prichard (*Mind*, 1912) | pairs with Bradley's Essay II |
| Ring of Gyges | ✅ Plato, *Republic* II 359d–360b | the canonical statement of the "all the time" problem |
| Hume's sensible knave | ✅ *Enquiry Concerning the Principles of Morals*, IX.ii | Hume's own difficulty, honestly admitted |
| intrinsic/extrinsic religiosity | ✅ Gordon Allport; quest orientation ✅ Daniel Batson | the apparatus for the 2018 stem |
| *Ressentiment* (the critique of Nietzsche) | ✅ Max Scheler (1912) | Christian love from abundance, not impotence |
| *Gay Science* §125; *Genealogy* I–III; *BGE* Preface | ✅ verified locations | paraphrase the rhetoric; quote sparingly |
| Aquinas' three war conditions | ✅ *ST* II-II q.40 | authority, just cause, right intention |
| *Just and Unjust Wars* | ✅ Michael Walzer (1977) | modern *ad bellum*/*in bello* systematisation |
| "greater/lesser *jihād*" | ❓ report of disputed authenticity among ḥadīth scholars | cite **with** the flag; never as settled doctrine |
| *dār al-islām*/*dār al-ḥarb* | ⚠️ juristic-historical constructs, not Qur'ānic terms | say so explicitly |
| *dharma-yuddha* rules | ✅ *Mahābhārata* (*Śānti-*/*Anuśāsana-parvan*) and Dharmaśāstra | give the protected categories, then the internal tension |
| *kūṭa-yuddha* | ✅ Kauṭilya's *Arthaśāstra* | the Indian realist counter-tradition |
| *compelle intrare* (Luke 14:23) | ✅ Augustine's Donatist-controversy use | examined as a position, never endorsed |
| Locke on the incoercibility of belief | ✅ *A Letter Concerning Toleration* (1689) | the decisive refutation of soteriological coercion |
| Śāntideva on ownerless suffering | ✅ *Bodhicaryāvatāra*, ch. VIII | the strongest non-theistic grounding of impartial altruism |
| *Gītā* 2.3 (*klaibya*), 2.42–43, 2.47, 3.19 | ✅ verified chapter and verse | paraphrase translations |

**[FACT] The five foundational statements.** (1) ✅ The Euthyphro dilemma asks whether divine approval constitutes goodness or responds to goodness. (2) ❓ The sentence commonly rendered as "If God does not exist, everything is permitted" is a summary of a Dostoevskian theme, not a safe verbatim quotation. (3) ✅ Kant grounds moral obligation in rational autonomy and relates religion to the requirements of practical reason. (4) ✅ James's pragmatic test directs attention to the fruits of religious life. (5) ⚠️ Indian *dharma* frequently combines moral, ritual, social and soteriological dimensions; it is not reducible to any one of them.

**[FACT] The corpus signal.** ⚠️ Primary ownership is 11 of 112 audited question-parts, including three distinct demands in 2019. ✅ The required doctrinal depth is set by the older questions, which add the printed religiosity–"immorality" relation, religious violence, conflict with social morality, secular motivation, the compatibility of belief and practice with conduct, and inseparability claims. ❌ The answer consequence: do not reduce the debate to divine command theory; distinguish conceptual dependence, epistemic grounding, motivational force and actual sociological influence.

**[INFERENCE] The one-sentence thesis of the whole clause.** Religion and morality are **conceptually distinguishable, historically interactive and normatively mutually critical**. Each of the three adjectives corresponds to a block of the package: distinguishable is Sessions 1–7 and 11–13; interactive is Sessions 8–10 and 14–17; mutually critical is Sessions 9, 16, 18–20. If a candidate can defend all three adjectives with named evidence, every printed stem in the corpus is answerable.

**Objection.** A synthesis of this size cannot be deployed in a fifteen-minute answer, so it is examination-irrelevant however true it is.
**Reply.** **[INFERENCE]** The synthesis is not written to be *deployed*; it is written to be *selected from*. The decoder tells you which of the four relations the stem asserts; the bench supplies two or three rows, not nine; the ladder tells you the one discrimination that lifts the band; and the architecture tells you the shape. What is deployed is roughly one-fifth of what is here, and knowing which fifth is exactly what the synthesis is for.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH

> **Claim:** The soundest overall position is the interaction view: morality is autonomous at the level of grounding while remaining historically and motivationally entangled with religion, and each criticises the other.
> **Named evidence:** The Euthyphro blocks the reduction of morality to divine command; four independent non-theistic groundings — Kantian autonomy, Mīmāṃsā's injunction, Śāntideva's ownerless suffering and the Jain vow-structure — confirm that obligation can be grounded without God; while narrative exemplars, ritual formation, community sanction, hope and a sustaining worldview show religion's real motivational contribution, and the prophetic critique shows morality's real corrective work on religion.
> **Analysis:** Because autonomy is established at the level of grounding and dependence survives only at the level of motivation and history, the correct thesis is relational rather than reductive in either direction, which is exactly what "conceptually distinguishable, historically interactive and normatively mutually critical" asserts.
> **Qualification:** The interaction itself is contingent — the same mechanisms that sustain care can sanction exclusion — so the verdict must be graded rather than celebratory, and the concession that costs something is that religion's motivational advantage in the undetected case is genuine even though its grounding claim fails.

#### UPSC TRAPS

1. ⚠️ Do not write the synthesis out. Select from it: two bench rows, one discrimination, one concession and a decided verdict is a stronger script than a compressed catalogue.
2. ⚠️ Do not let the verdict summarise. The ladder's "Strong" band fails to reach "Top" precisely because the verdict restates rather than decides; a decided verdict names what would change it.
3. ⚠️ Do not omit the concession that costs your own position something. It is the single most reliable marker of the top band in this clause.

#### MINI RECAP

Grid for tens, bench for fifteens and twenties. Decoder: ten directives, ten demands. Ladder: five bands, and the top one requires a discrimination plus a costly concession. Architectures: three shapes. Traps: fifteen. Thesis: distinguishable, interactive, mutually critical.

---

## BASIC MCQS / REMEDIATION

### Practice protocol

Attempt every item before reading its key. The correct option position was fixed **independently for each question** by a randomised, length-balanced assignment: there is no rotation, no repeating cycle and no adjacent repetition to exploit, and each of the four positions carries exactly eight answers across the thirty-two diagnostics. Every option carries a question-specific explanation, and every question carries a unique examiner trap.

#### CORE DIAGNOSTIC MCQS — 24

#### MCQ 1. Which relation is being asserted?

A candidate writes that "morality depends on religion". Which statement expresses the **metaphysical grounding** version of that claim rather than one of the other three?

A. Religious communities transmit moral habits more effectively than isolated individuals form them, so moral formation is a communal achievement.

B. Belief that conduct is finally judged raises compliance in exactly those cases where detection by other people is improbable or impossible.

C. The validity of a moral obligation is constituted by a religious fact, such as the command of God, so that removing the fact removes the obligation itself.

D. Moral reformers have historically drawn their vocabulary of dignity, justice and conscience from the religious traditions they were criticising.

**Answer: C.**

**Option explanations:**

- **A:** Incorrect: this is a claim about moral formation and socialisation, which belongs to religion's *influence* on conduct and settles nothing about what makes an obligation valid.
- **B:** Incorrect: this is the **sanction and accountability** relation — what connects conduct to consequence — and it is compatible with obligation being grounded entirely elsewhere.
- **C:** Correct: grounding asks what *constitutes* validity, and the constitution test is exactly the one stated — remove the religious fact and the obligation goes with it.
- **D:** Incorrect: this is a genealogical or historical point about vocabulary, and where a concept came from is a different question from what now makes it binding.

**Examiner trap 1:** A script that never says which of the four relations it is discussing cannot be graded above the average band, however much doctrine it contains; name the relation in the opening sentence and hold to it.

#### MCQ 2. What behavioural evidence can and cannot establish

Suppose it is well attested that religious participation correlates with higher charitable giving. What follows philosophically?

A. Divine command theory survives the Euthyphro dilemma, because a theory with such consequences cannot be the arbitrary doctrine its critics allege.

B. Those who do not participate in religious practice cannot know that charity is morally required of them or of anyone else.

C. Charitable giving is obligatory only in so far as it is commanded, since the correlation reveals the true source of the obligation.

D. Religion may causally influence conduct, but neither logical dependence nor any conclusion about moral truth follows from the correlation.

**Answer: D.**

**Option explanations:**

- **A:** Incorrect: the Euthyphro is a conceptual objection to a theory of grounding, and no volume of behavioural evidence bears on whether a command constitutes obligation.
- **B:** Incorrect: this confuses causal influence with epistemic access, and it is refuted by the ordinary fact that non-participants make and defend the same moral judgement.
- **C:** Incorrect: this is precisely the inference the correlation cannot support, since a cause of compliance is not a constituent of obligation.
- **D:** Correct: correlation establishes at most a contingent influence, and may itself require controls for community, selection and orientation before even that is secure.

**Examiner trap 2:** The correlation trap is the commonest single failure in this clause because the evidence really is good; concede the influence generously and then state, in the same sentence, the inference it cannot support.

#### MCQ 3. How divine command theory should be stated

Which formulation states the thesis that the Euthyphro dilemma is actually aimed at?

A. The word "wrong" simply means "forbidden by God", so an atheist who calls an act wrong is not using the term in the primary sense it bears.

B. Moral obligation is constituted by God's command, so that being commanded is what the obligatoriness of an act consists in.

C. Belief in a commanding God is what in practice moves most people to act morally when acting morally is costly to them.

D. Religion supplies moral education, so that without religious teaching most people would never acquire the concepts morality requires.

**Answer: B.**

**Option explanations:**

- **A:** Incorrect: this is the **semantic** version, the strongest and least defensible reading, and almost no defender of the theory holds it; refuting it proves very little.
- **B:** Correct: the metaphysical version is the real thesis — command is constitutive of obligation, not evidential, motivational or reinforcing — and it is the version the dilemma targets.
- **C:** Incorrect: this is the **motivational** version; it may well be true of many agents and it says nothing about what makes an act obligatory.
- **D:** Incorrect: this is a claim about moral pedagogy, and a theory of moral education cannot be attacked by a dilemma about the constitution of obligation.

**Examiner trap 3:** Attacking the semantic version and announcing that divine command theory has been refuted is a straw-man move an examiner will see immediately; aim at the constitutive claim.

#### MCQ 4. The presupposition Kant denies

Divine command theory rests on three presuppositions. Which one does the Kantian reject while leaving the other two untouched?

A. That authority as such can generate moral reasons, so that a command issued by a legitimate superior binds the agent by itself.

B. That God exists as a supreme personal being capable of issuing commands that apply universally to every rational agent whatever.

C. That divine commands are knowable, so that fallible human agents can reliably identify what has in fact been commanded of them in a given case.

D. That divine knowledge and goodness secure the rightness of the content commanded, so that the commands will never be perverse.

**Answer: A.**

**Option explanations:**

- **A:** Correct: Kant's objection is that a law given by another will is heteronomous, so authority as such cannot generate a genuinely moral reason however legitimate the authority is.
- **B:** Incorrect: existence is contested by the atheist, not by the Kantian *as* a Kantian; Kant retains God as a practical postulate rather than denying the being.
- **C:** Incorrect: the knowability worry is an epistemic objection pressed hardest in the religious-violence module, and it is not the distinctively Kantian complaint.
- **D:** Incorrect: the content-guarantee is challenged by the Euthyphro's arbitrariness horn, which is a different objection from the autonomy objection at issue here.

**Examiner trap 4:** Candidates routinely present Kant as an atheistic critic; the sharper and more accurate presentation is that Kant grants the theist his God and still denies that a command can ground obligation.

#### MCQ 5. The dilemma in both horns

Which pair correctly states the two horns of the Euthyphro dilemma and the cost of each?

A. Either God exists and morality is objective, or God does not exist and morality is a human convention with no genuine claim on anyone at all.

B. Either goodness is constituted by the command, which makes morality arbitrary, or the command follows goodness, which makes goodness independent.

C. Either revelation discloses moral truth reliably, or moral truth is inaccessible, in which case no one can be blamed for moral failure at all.

D. Either God is omnipotent and evil is unreal, or evil is real and God's power over the created order must be significantly limited.

**Answer: B.**

**Option explanations:**

- **A:** Incorrect: this is the objectivity-versus-conventionalism debate, not the Euthyphro, which is entirely available to a theist and does not turn on God's existence.
- **B:** Correct: this is the dilemma as Plato states it at *Euthyphro* 10a, with the arbitrariness cost on the first horn and the independence cost on the second.
- **C:** Incorrect: this concerns epistemic access to moral truth and belongs to the reason-and-revelation clause rather than to the constitution of obligation.
- **D:** Incorrect: this is the problem of evil, which shares Plato's dialogical form in some presentations but has an entirely different target.

**Examiner trap 5:** A script that gives one horn has given half a dilemma, which is not a dilemma at all; the second horn is what an examiner scans for to test whether the structure is understood.

#### MCQ 6. The consequence that strengthens the first horn

Beyond arbitrariness, what further damage does the first horn of the Euthyphro do to the theist's own position?

A. It shows that God could not have created a world containing genuinely free agents capable of moral responsibility.

B. It shows that revelation must be superfluous, since a command that constitutes goodness could never be misunderstood by its recipients.

C. It shows that moral knowledge would have to be innate, since no experience could disclose what a bare act of will has instituted.

D. It empties the affirmation of divine goodness, since the claim that God is good collapses into the tautology that God commands what God commands.

**Answer: D.**

**Option explanations:**

- **A:** Incorrect: free will and moral responsibility are the terrain of the problem of evil and of theological determinism, not a consequence of the arbitrariness horn.
- **B:** Incorrect: nothing in the horn implies that commands are self-interpreting; misidentification of the divine will is a separate and independently pressed objection.
- **C:** Incorrect: innateness is a thesis in moral epistemology and does not follow from a claim about what constitutes goodness.
- **D:** Correct: if goodness just is what God commands, the central theistic affirmation of divine goodness becomes vacuous, so the horn threatens theology as well as ethics.

**Examiner trap 6:** Stopping at arbitrariness is what separates the average band from the good band here; the vacuity consequence is short to state and it is the point examiners reward.

#### MCQ 7. What Adams actually changed

What is Robert Merrihew Adams's central modification of divine command theory?

A. Obligation is grounded in the commands of an essentially loving God, while goodness is grounded in resemblance to the divine nature.

B. Moral obligation is wholly reducible to the anticipation of divine reward and the fear of divine punishment in a life after death.

C. Moral goodness is whatever a recognised religious authority declares it to be, provided the authority is legitimately constituted within its tradition.

D. Divine commands are unnecessary to ethics, because the consequences of an action are the only thing that determines whether it ought to be done.

**Answer: A.**

**Option explanations:**

- **A:** Correct: the split is the move — obligation from command, goodness from resemblance to nature — and it is what blocks the arbitrariness horn without abandoning the theory.
- **B:** Incorrect: this reduces the theory to a prudential sanction account, which is the version Kant's heteronomy objection destroys and which Adams is not defending.
- **C:** Incorrect: this substitutes ecclesiastical or institutional authority for the divine nature and reintroduces exactly the arbitrariness the modification exists to prevent.
- **D:** Incorrect: this is a consequentialist thesis, and Adams's theory is a form of divine command theory rather than a rejection of one.

**Examiner trap 7:** Writing "Adams says God is loving so the problem disappears" earns nothing; the mark is for the *split* between the ground of obligation and the ground of goodness.

#### MCQ 8. The reply that blocks the regress

Which position holds that God is the supreme standard of goodness rather than a being who conforms to an external standard, on the model of the standard metre bar?

A. Linda Zagzebski's divine motivation theory, which grounds moral value in God's motivational states rather than in God's legislative acts.

B. Immanuel Kant's doctrine of the practical postulates, which secures the coherence of the moral outlook without grounding the moral law in God.

C. William Alston's standard reply, which denies that there must be a further standard the divine nature has to match in order to be good.

D. The natural-law tradition, which holds that basic human goods are disclosed by inclination and directed to their ends by practical reason.

**Answer: C.**

**Option explanations:**

- **A:** Incorrect: Zagzebski relocates the ground from commands to motives, which converts the theory into a virtue ethics but does not deploy the standard-metre analogy.
- **B:** Incorrect: the postulates concern what practical reason must presuppose, and Kant's whole point is that God is not the source of the moral law at all.
- **C:** Correct: Alston's reply denies the assumed regress by treating God as the standard itself, so the demand for a further standard misconstrues what a standard is.
- **D:** Incorrect: natural law grounds the order in divine reason and gives reason the access, but it does not answer the regress by an appeal to a paradigm standard.

**Examiner trap 8:** The standard-metre analogy is regularly misattributed to Adams; keeping Adams's split and Alston's standard distinct is a cheap and visible precision gain.

#### MCQ 9. What the modified theory concedes

What is the strongest residual objection to modified divine command theory?

A. Describing the divine nature as loving deploys a moral concept whose content we must already grasp independently of the theory itself.

B. It denies that God can be loving, and therefore contradicts the central affirmation that every theistic tradition makes about the divine character.

C. It establishes that all moral values are ultimately subjective, since a nature is a psychological rather than a normative kind of thing.

D. It makes moral obligation impossible for anyone who lacks access to a revealed text in which the relevant commands are recorded and transmitted.

**Answer: A.**

**Option explanations:**

- **A:** Correct: this is the residue — non-arbitrariness is secured at the cost of conceding conceptual independence to goodness, which is what the second horn asserts.
- **B:** Incorrect: the theory affirms rather than denies divine love; the objection is about the *concept* of love, not about whether the attribute is possessed.
- **C:** Incorrect: nothing in the objection entails subjectivism, and the theory is a form of moral realism about both goodness and obligation.
- **D:** Incorrect: this is the access objection, which is pressed against command theories generally and is not the distinctive residue of the modification.

**Examiner trap 9:** Presenting the modified theory as a clean victory over the Euthyphro reads as advocacy; the evaluative verdict is *relocation*, not refutation, and it must be stated as such.

#### MCQ 10. The fork that decides moral worth

On Kant's account, what is the decisive defect of an act of truth-telling performed only from fear of divine punishment?

A. It rests on a factual belief about the afterlife that cannot be established by any argument that theoretical reason is competent to run.

B. It cannot be universalised, since a maxim of truth-telling motivated by fear would be self-contradictory when made a law of nature.

C. It treats the person deceived merely as a means, and therefore violates the requirement that humanity always also be treated as an end.

D. It is heteronomous: the law is received from another will and made conditional on a desired outcome, so the act lacks moral worth.

**Answer: D.**

**Option explanations:**

- **A:** Incorrect: Kant is not objecting to the belief's epistemic credentials here; he objects to the structure of the motivation whatever the belief's status.
- **B:** Incorrect: the maxim of truth-telling universalises perfectly well; it is the *motive*, not the maxim's form, that Kant is assessing in this example.
- **C:** Incorrect: the humanity formula concerns how others are treated, whereas the defect in this case concerns the ground on which the agent acts.
- **D:** Correct: obligation received from another will and made conditional on reward or fear is heteronomous, and heteronomy is what deprives the act of moral worth.

**Examiner trap 10:** The example is often reported as Kant objecting to religion; he is objecting to *sanction as a motive*, and the difference is the whole content of the point.

#### MCQ 11. Where God enters Kant's practical philosophy

What role do God, freedom and immortality play in Kant's moral philosophy?

A. They are the three premises from which the categorical imperative is derived by an argument of practical reason.

B. They are the objects of a revelation which practical reason may accept on authority once it has exhausted its own resources.

C. They are practical postulates: what reason must presuppose for the moral outlook to be coherent, and not the source of the moral law itself.

D. They are theoretical conclusions established by the arguments of the *Critique of Pure Reason* and then applied to moral questions.

**Answer: C.**

**Option explanations:**

- **A:** Incorrect: the categorical imperative is derived from the form of rational willing, and nothing about God enters the derivation as a premise.
- **B:** Incorrect: nothing is accepted on authority here; the postulates are demands of practical reason and are not items of revealed content.
- **C:** Correct: they are presuppositions of the moral outlook, which is why Kant relates religion to morality without making God the ground of obligation.
- **D:** Incorrect: the first *Critique* denies precisely that theoretical reason can establish these, which is why they are postulated practically instead.

**Examiner trap 11:** "Kant removes God from morality" is a half-truth that loses marks; what is removed is God's role as the *ground* of obligation, and the postulates remain.

#### MCQ 12. The mediating claim

Which sentence best captures why natural law occupies the middle ground between divine command theory and Kantian autonomy?

A. It grounds obligation in consequences while retaining the vocabulary of divine purpose, so it satisfies the theist without incurring metaphysical commitments.

B. It denies that morality has any objective ground, and treats religious and secular moral languages as two equally serviceable ways of speaking.

C. It makes revelation the sole source of moral knowledge while conceding that God's commands are constrained by his own nature.

D. God grounds the objective order of human goods, but practical reason knows much of that order without any appeal to revelation.

**Answer: D.**

**Option explanations:**

- **A:** Incorrect: natural law is not consequentialist; basic goods are ends disclosed to practical reason, not outcomes to be maximised.
- **B:** Incorrect: natural law is strongly objectivist about the order of goods, and the description offered here is a relativism it explicitly rejects.
- **C:** Incorrect: the position's distinctive claim is precisely that much of the moral order is accessible *without* revelation, which this option reverses.
- **D:** Correct: God supplies the objectivity of the order and reason supplies the access, which is exactly what makes the position mediate between the two rivals.

**Examiner trap 12:** Collapsing natural law into divine command theory is the standard error; the discriminator is order-versus-decree and reason-versus-revelation, and one sentence fixes it.

#### MCQ 13. The objection natural law must answer

What is the standard objection to natural law, and what is the standard reply?

A. Objection: it makes God superfluous. Reply: divine reason is required to explain why the order of goods is objective rather than a human construction.

B. Objection: it derives a norm from a fact about human nature. Reply: practical reason grasps goods *as reasons*, so the starting point is already normative in itself.

C. Objection: it cannot explain moral disagreement. Reply: disagreement is explained by the varying degrees of participation in the eternal law across cultures.

D. Objection: it is too demanding. Reply: the theory grades obligations by vocation, so no agent is required to pursue every basic good simultaneously.

**Answer: B.**

**Option explanations:**

- **A:** Incorrect: this reverses the direction of the standard complaint, which is that nature-to-norm inference fails, not that the divine grounding is idle.
- **B:** Correct: the naturalistic-fallacy worry is the standing objection, and the goods-as-reasons reply denies that anything normative is being derived from a bare description.
- **C:** Incorrect: moral disagreement is a general problem for every objectivist theory and is not the objection specifically pressed against natural law.
- **D:** Incorrect: demandingness is the standard complaint against utilitarianism; graded vows belong to the Jain structure, not to natural law.

**Examiner trap 13:** Naming an objection without its reply reads as a checklist; naming the reply and then conceding that it is defensible rather than decisive reads as evaluation.

#### MCQ 14. Matching the secular families to their bills

Which pairing of a secular grounding with its standing objection is correct?

A. Virtue ethics — the perfect defector who is never detected has been given no reason at all to keep the agreement he has made.

B. Utilitarianism — it presupposes a thick account of human nature which a sceptic is entitled simply to decline to share with the theorist.

C. Contractarianism — the disposition-argument works only where dispositions are detectable, so the masked knave is unanswered.

D. Humanism — it can override individual rights whenever aggregate welfare is thereby increased, and it may demand more than agents can give.

**Answer: C.**

**Option explanations:**

- **A:** Incorrect: the undetected defector is the contractarian's difficulty; the virtue theorist's problem is the thickness of the nature his account presupposes.
- **B:** Incorrect: the thick-nature objection belongs to eudaimonism; utilitarianism's standing problems are rights-override and demandingness.
- **C:** Correct: constrained maximisation shows that being disposed to keep agreements maximises utility only where the disposition can be recognised by others.
- **D:** Incorrect: rights-override and demandingness are utilitarian bills; the humanist's difficulty is explaining the source of the worth of persons.

**Examiner trap 14:** Writing "secular ethics" as one lump concedes the field to the theist; naming two families and their distinct bills is the cheapest available upgrade on this stem.

#### MCQ 15. What the interaction view can and cannot claim

Which statement correctly describes the limits of the interaction view?
